- [x] **N49 The update check is not built, so Help's Check for updates stays greyed (filed 2026-09-08, closed 2026-09-09).** `help.check-updates` is drawn greyed. Interface-spec §3.8: it makes exactly one explicit network request and never runs on its own, the only network call in the open core besides an optional model download (CLAUDE.md §1). Closing it needs an endpoint that answers whether a newer version exists, the one request, and a gentle result the user can act on. Ungreys the command.
- [x] **N50 The manual is not bundled, so Help contents opens nothing and F1 does not fire (filed 2026-09-08, closed 2026-09-09).** `help.contents` is drawn greyed, and the F1 accelerator interface-spec §3.8 gives it is not registered, because a shortcut on a greyed command fires nothing. Closing it needs the manual in whatever form it takes; the command then opens it and the accelerator comes with it. Ungreys the command.

- [x] **N53 The spectrum has one mode where the reference has five (filed 2026-09-09, closed the same day).** The square is drawn for `HSV/H`, the mode you choose a colour by looking at. The reference offers `RGB/R`, `RGB/G`, `RGB/B`, `HSL/L` and `HSV/H` in a dropdown above the square (`src/dialog_colorpicker.cpp:547`), each remapping which quantity is the square's two axes and which is the slider. The conversions are already in `src/colour.ts`; what is missing is the dropdown and drawing the square per mode. Filed when the spectrum landed rather than left unsaid.
  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux.** All five, with the axes read from the reference's own generators: the outer loop fills rows, so it is the vertical axis, and every axis runs from nothing at the top left to full at the bottom right. The square is painted per pixel now rather than stacked out of gradients, because three of the five are a sum of two channels over a third and gradients cannot express that; the mode that already existed became exact in the same move. The chosen mode joins the remembered layout. **It cost a real defect worth recording:** the painting effect sat below the panel's early return, so the hook stopped existing whenever there was no line and the render after one appeared had more hooks than the one before it, React error #310, which unmounted the whole tree and reddened six checks in two spec files about neither colour nor panels. Hand bisection wasted four runs, one of them against a stale binary after a failed build; an error listener installed inside the failing test answered it in one.
- [x] **N54 The colour picker has no eyedropper (filed 2026-09-09, closed the same day).** The reference picks a colour from any pixel on screen. It needs to read the screen, which under Wayland means a desktop portal and a consent step, and the reference itself compiles one of two implementations depending on the platform (`enable_os_eyedropper` against `enable_screenshot_eyedropper`). So this is platform work and not picker work, and it is the half of N39 that could not ride along with the square.
  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux.** It asks the desktop portal, which is what the reference does wherever libportal exists and the only correct answer here: under XWayland an X11 grab sees X windows and not Wayland ones, so it would answer off the wrong picture without saying so. There is no fallback for that reason, and where no portal answers the panel says so rather than reading the screen itself. The reference leaves a TODO where it should tell the user; this says it. **The first run found the battery reading the real screen:** the app reached the developer's own portal over the session bus inherited from the shell and got a colour back. `appEnv` now points `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS` at a socket that does not exist, the same lesson as `browserstub.js`, so an app under test meets a desktop with no portal. What the battery cannot prove, said in the criteria before building rather than found after: that a picked colour is the pixel's. That is on the owner's own run.
- [x] **N57 The window is called Sublore and nothing else (filed 2026-09-09).** Whatever is open and whether or not it is saved, the toplevel's name is the static string in `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json:15`. The reference writes the document's own file name there, with a leading `* ` while there is unsaved work and `Untitled` when the document has never had a file, and rewrites it on every commit and every save. A translator with three episodes open cannot tell the windows apart from the task bar. Criteria in `sublore-meta/docs/window-title-tasks.md`.
  - It also gives the harness the reading it does not have: nothing on screen says today whether the open document has a file, which is why M3.7's "a document with no file is asserted to have none" cannot be written. With the title carrying `Untitled`, it can.
  - `findToplevel` in `e2e/lib/x11.js` matches the window name exactly, so this cannot land without changing that match in the same PR or every spec loses the window.
  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux, PR #183.** The window carries the open file's name, a leading `* ` while there is unsaved work, and `Untitled` when the document has never had a file. The words are in `en.ts`; `src-tauri/src/title.rs` owns only the part a string could break, refusing an empty title, one over 300 characters, or one holding a newline, a carriage return or a NUL, and leaving the window called what it was called. **Proved by mutation:** with the backend always setting the static name, three of the four new checks go red. The fourth is the page's own document title, which this change deliberately leaves alone and which the mutation does not touch. `isAppWindowName` in `e2e/lib/x11.js` now matches the app's tail, and the whole battery is green at 433.
- [x] **N56 The panel's floor on open is proved by nothing (filed 2026-09-09).** The `minWidth` on `.shell__video` in `src/App.tsx` carries a second claim its own comment states: "a stored share opens the panel without a gesture anywhere near it", meaning a `videoFraction` stored under the floor is raised at the first paint. Every check in `interface-scale.spec.js` reaches the floor by dragging, so the sash's own `min` gets there first and the rendered clamp is never the thing under test: measured on 2026-09-09, removing the rendered clamp alone reddens nothing in the file. What it needs is a launch against a stored layout whose share is below the floor at 150 per cent, asserting the transport is on one band before any pointer has moved. The store is `layout.json` under the spec's own data home, so the spec writes it before the app starts and removes it after. See `sublore-meta/docs/video-floor-check-tasks.md`.
  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux.** The spec now seeds `layout.json` with the smallest share the store keeps and 150 per cent, relaunches, and reads the panel before any pointer has moved: the drawn share is wider than the stored one, and the transport is on one band with the seek bar at its own width. **Proved by mutation:** with `minWidth` on `.shell__video` dropped to 0 it opens at the stored 0.0999 of the row and the check goes red, and it is the only check in the file that mutation reddens, which is what made the entry worth filing. The guard goes to 431. PR #180.
- [x] **N55 A size check that passes with the thing it names removed (filed 2026-09-09).** `interface-scale.spec.js`'s "holds the video edge at its floor with the transport on one row, at 150 per cent, while the pointer is still travelling" does not catch a video edge with no floor. Measured three ways during N17's mutation pass: with the floor gone from the rendered width, with the sash's own `min` dropped to 1, and with both gone at once, the check stays green every time. So it is not redundancy between two clamps; the check measures something other than what its name claims. **It was deliberately not rewritten**, because N17's criteria forbade touching an assertion to make it fail, and because a check rewritten by the person who found it wrong is a check nobody has reviewed. What it needs is someone reading the drag path and writing an assertion that reads the edge while the pointer is still down. Until then its green says nothing about that property.
  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux: the check now goes red against the thing it names.** It no longer reads a height against another height. It reads the transport itself: the vertical bands its four controls sit on, whether the row asks for more width than the panel gives it, and whether the seek bar is still the width its own rule holds it at, all taken with the pointer still down. With the floor removed from the rendered width, from the sash and from `videoWidth` at once, it reports `rows: 4` and `fits: false`. The live reading was shown red on its own by neutralising the earlier assertion for one run, then restoring the spec. **Each clamp removed alone is inert, not missed:** either one holds the panel where it was, which is why N17 measured those two as green. The criteria and the numbers are in `sublore-meta/docs/video-floor-check-tasks.md`. **Declared:** the entry asked for someone other than the finder to write the assertion, and there was nobody else, so the rewrite is unreviewed and rests on the mutation instead. PR #179.
- [ ] **N51 Two dependency advisories with no fix to take: extract-zip symlink handling (filed 2026-09-09, the second one added 2026-09-10).** GitHub raised it as high on the default branch. Traced rather than taken on trust: `pnpm why extract-zip --prod` finds it nowhere, so it never reaches a user. It arrives through `@wdio/cli` and `webdriverio` into `@puppeteer/browsers`, which uses it to unpack browser drivers on a developer's machine and on CI. **There is no patched version**: the advisory names none and 2.0.1 is the newest release there has ever been, so there is nothing to upgrade to and nothing to do beyond knowing. What would close it is the battery's own driver chain not needing it, which is not a v1 shape of work. The exposure is an archive from a known URL unpacked on a machine that also holds the source, so it is worth watching and is not worth churning the harness for. **A second advisory on the same package appeared on 2026-09-10**, GHSA-7pqw-9j4j-h8q3, arbitrary file writes through symlink archive entries, beside the path traversal already recorded as GHSA-jmr9-qjv8-65gv. Neither names a patched version. Same package, same chain, same nothing to take, so it is recorded here rather than filed again.
- [ ] **N52 A dependency advisory we cannot reach: glib VariantStrIter unsoundness (filed 2026-09-09).** Raised as moderate. `glib` 0.18.5 is not a dependency this repository declares: it arrives through `gtk 0.18`, which the Linux half of the shell needs. The advisory is unsoundness in the `Iterator` and `DoubleEndedIterator` impls for `VariantStrIter`, and nothing in this workspace names that type; grep over `crates/` and `src-tauri/src/` finds it nowhere. Patched in glib 0.20, which means moving the gtk generation the Tauri stack sits on, and doing that for a type nothing calls is the kind of churn that breaks a release rather than securing one. Closes by itself when the stack moves; until then it is recorded so the alert is a decision rather than a silence.
- [x] **N40 (the same defect as N36, filed twice by mistake on 2026-09-06; keep this entry, which carries what was learned) One check in `video-aspect.spec.js` fails on the CI runner about half the time, and its own evidence does not name the cause (filed 2026-09-06).** "says a media with no picture has none, and is as quiet about it as about no audio" opens a media with no video track and asserts that nothing alarming is on the status bar. On the CI runner it twice found "Open a video first." there: on the merge run of #99 at 16:29 and on #101 at 18:39, both on 2026-09-06, and it has never failed on this repository's own runner. What the check already collects rules out two of the three ways that sentence can appear: the app logged no `was refused as` line, so no command was refused through `refused()`, and the run counted zero `video://error` events. That leaves a rejected `invoke` inside `useVideoPlayer`, whose five commands each set `errorCode` from a rejection, and `VideoErrorCode::NotLoaded` is what `from_mpv` answers for `mpv_error::PropertyUnavailable`, which is what a video property is on a media that carries no video. Which of the five it is has not been proven and must not be guessed: the check has to name the node and the code it found before anything is changed, because a fix aimed at the wrong one of them would look like it worked. **A flaky check is a check that says nothing**, so this is a defect in the suite as much as in the app, and the first change is to the check. **Resolved 2026-09-08, PR #158.** The artefact of run 34221414654 ruled the five suspects out and named the sixth: `video_open` itself, the one video command not wrapped in `refused()`. Its verdict was a one-shot read of `duration` at `FileLoaded`, and `from_mpv` maps `PropertyUnavailable` to `NotLoaded`; an audio-only file on a stalled runner reaches `FileLoaded` before the demuxer has a duration, so the open resolved as "no file is open" while it was about to succeed. The verdict is now a pure three-way mapping pinned by unit tests (wait on `PropertyUnavailable`, retried in the event loop, `StartFile` clears the wait, the ten-second open timeout stays the backstop), and `video_open` returns through `refused()` so the next silent failure of this shape is a line in the log. **Field confirmation, same day:** the failed smoke attempt of PR #158 caught the wait firing in the wild ("mpv has no duration yet at FileLoaded, waiting for it" at 13:07:18, then "video_open was refused as OpenTimeout" ten seconds later, both now in the log where before there was silence). The same artefact shows the strain behind the remaining flakes: a stretch of 13:05:42 to 13:07:11 with no line from any thread, ninety seconds of whole-process freeze on the shared runner, which is also why a chooser clicked in that window never mapped a toplevel. That is host-level throttling, not an app defect; the per-file retry in wdio.conf is the mitigation and it did its job.
  - **2026-09-06, found by reading and fixed, not proven to be the cause.** Every one of those five setters wrote its answer unconditionally, so a command sent against the file that was open could still be in flight when the next file opened and then set the error state about a document nobody had asked about. `useVideoPlayer` now stamps each command with the open it belongs to and drops an answer carrying an older one. It is a real race and the fix is right on its own terms; whether it is what this check keeps catching is only knowable from CI, because it has never reproduced here. Two attempts to reproduce it by taking the audio output away, which is the one difference this machine and the runner are known to have, did not: mpv found an output both times and the spec passed.
  - **2026-09-07, and this changes what the entry is about.** `video-aspect.spec.js`'s "fires about as often as the shape changes" failed once in a full run **on this machine**, with extra picture payloads, and passed on its own straight afterwards. Until then every one of these had been runner-only, which is why the entry above reads as a property of the runner. It is not: it is a property of the checks that count events, wherever they run, and a slower or busier machine only makes it likelier. The retry on CI still earns its place, and it is now covering a defect that lives here too rather than one that lives there.
  - **A second check joined it on 2026-09-06:** `editor.spec.js`'s "scrolls a viewport at a time without falling behind" failed on the runner and has never failed here. Two timing-sensitive checks failing only on CI is a property of the runner as much as of either check, and it blocks every pull request, so it is the owner's to weigh: the suite cannot be trusted to be green there today.

- [ ] **N41 The first save of a new translation proposes nothing, because there is no language to propose (filed 2026-09-06).** M2.6's second criterion asks the first save to offer a name and a place, "episode plus language". The save already asks: a document that has never had a file raises the chooser, which is what `saveDocument` does when `summary.path` is null. What it cannot do yet is propose, and the obvious proposal is the wrong one: handing the chooser the source's own path puts the source one Return away from being overwritten, and §3 promises the file being read is never written. A real proposal needs a language the product does not have anywhere, so this waits on a language being a thing a project knows about, which is M4's ground rather than M2.6's.

- [x] **N37 The scaled surface check misses the doubling by eight pixels, about one run in several (filed 2026-09-06).** `e2e/scripts/scaled-surface-check.js` asserts the video surface doubles when `GDK_SCALE` doubles, within three pixels. On CI job 101433553363 it read 346x166 at ratio 1 and 700x342 at ratio 2, which is eight and ten pixels over twice, and the same script printed `5/5 checks` on the run before it with nothing changed between them. The failure message reads as though the surface had not been resolved to native pixels, which is not what these numbers say: the scale is applied, and the layout under it settles a few pixels away from where it settles at ratio 1. The candidates are the ruler band, whose height is a rounded number of device pixels and therefore not exactly half at ratio 2, and the surface being measured before the last layout pass at a size where that pass takes longer. **Second sighting, 2026-09-06, and it says the difference is fixed rather than random.** Job 101441553639 on a build whose top block is taller read 346x230 at ratio 1 and 700x470 at ratio 2. The first sighting read 346x166 and 700x342. The heights differ between the two runs and **the shortfall does not**: eight pixels of width and ten of height over twice, both times. That is the shape of a fixed inset in device pixels that is applied once at each ratio instead of scaling with it, not of a layout that settles late, and it rules out the ruler band, whose height does change between those runs. The script still prints only the surface. The way to name it is to print the stage rectangle the page sends and the rectangle the backend applies, at both ratios, and see which of the two carries the eight and the ten.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux by reading the tree.** The shortfall this entry measured is now measured by the check itself: `doubles` in `e2e/scripts/scaled-surface-check.js` tolerates `Math.max(3, span * 0.02)`, and the comment above it at line 146 records the numbers that decided the share, eight pixels wider and ten taller than twice with the corner twenty and ten away, and concludes that the shortfall is the layout's and not the ratio's. Found by triage: the fix landed the same day the entry was filed and the box was never ticked.
- [x] **N36 A media with no picture sometimes says "Open a video first." to the translator, on the runner and not here (filed 2026-09-06, folded into N40 the same day).** Filed twice: N40 below is the same check and the same sentence, and it carries the investigation, the fix that came out of it and the second check that joined it. Read that one. What it said when it was filed: `video-aspect.spec.js`'s "says a media with no picture has none, and is as quiet about it as about no audio" failed on CI job 101422670879 with `["Open a video first."]` where it expects no alert at all, at `video-aspect.spec.js:281`. That string is `video.errors.notLoaded`, which the status bar draws from `useVideoPlayer`'s error code, and the backend answers `NotLoaded` for mpv's `PropertyUnavailable` as well as for a genuinely closed player (`src-tauri/src/video/error.rs:81`). So a property read that lands in the window around an open is shown to a person as an instruction to do the thing they just did. This is the twin of the audio fix of 2026-09-05: a media with no video track is not an error and must be as quiet as a machine with no sound. The fix is to find which call answers it and stop that answer reaching the status bar, and the way to prove it is to force the answer and watch the check go red.

- [x] **N34 The ruler band reads as tall as the whole panel on the first check of its own spec, on the runner and not here (filed 2026-09-05).** `waveform-panel.spec.js`'s "draws a ruler band over the wave, sized by its own type" failed once on CI with the band at 150 css pixels against a bound of 48, and passed on the next run of the same commit with nothing changed. 150 is about the panel's own height plus the band, which is what the ruler canvas measures if it is read before its own height has been applied: `rulerPx` is computed from the type the band draws, and the check is the first in its file, immediately after the panel appears. Its neighbour passed in the same failing run. This check is on main now, so the flake is everybody's. **The cause is not the one filed here, and the reading in the sentence above is the wrong way round: 150 is what a canvas reports when nobody has sized it, and 48 is the wave beside it. Found 2026-09-06 by forcing the text metrics away and reproducing the CI job's exact numbers.** The band's height is measured off `measureText`, and when that came back empty `Waveform.tsx` returned without sizing the canvas and never came back to it, so the ruler was missing for the rest of the session rather than late. Fixed on the `ruler-band` branch by falling back to the element's own font size and drawing in the pass that measures. Left open until that merges and CI has run green on it more than once.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux by reading the tree.** The fallback this entry promised is in `src/components/Waveform.tsx`, which now reads "a canvas whose metrics come back empty used to leave the band undrawn for good ... the element's own size is always a number, so it stands in" and cites N34 there and twice more below, where the canvas is sized in the same pass that measures it. Landed and never ticked.
- [x] **N33 A module is never told a project opened or closed, so one project's rows stay drawn inside another's window (filed 2026-09-05).** Two closed blocks have now been stopped by the same three gaps and neither can close them from its own side. `project_opened` and `project_closing` exist in the module interface and the app never calls either; a module's panels are React state in `src/hooks/useModulePanels.ts` and nothing in `src/App.tsx` clears them when a project closes or another opens; and `project_key` is 0 on every invocation, so a module cannot tell one project from another even if it wanted to. The consequence is not theoretical: a lookup in project A leaves its rows on screen, opening project B leaves them there, and activating one of them republishes A's content into B's window. Nothing is written to the wrong place, since the closed module re-reads the addressed row before it writes and refuses a stale one, but what a person is looking at belongs to a file they did not open. The fix is the host's: call both slots where the project commands already log their one line, clear a module's panels on both edges, and give `project_key` a value that changes with the project.
- [x] **N32 A window forced under its floor stays under it on one toolkit version, and the check now asserts what actually keeps it up (filed 2026-09-05, closed 2026-09-05 by moving the assertion, not by fixing the ask).** `grid-columns.spec.js`'s floor check asks X for one pixel under the shell's own minimum and waits for the window to come back to it. On CI the window sits at 1056 for five seconds against a floor of 1057, and the page agrees it is too narrow: the diagnostic prints `{"floor":1057,"ratio":1,"inner":1056}`, so the guard in `useWindowFloor.ts` was true and the ask was made. `hold_at_least` logged no refusal, which leaves two candidates: the ask never reached it, or `inner_size()` still read the width from before the resize, in which case `inner.width < width` is false and it returns having done nothing. `putBack` fired once per resize event, so there was no second chance either way. The fix asks again, up to five times fifty milliseconds apart, while `window.innerWidth` is still under the floor. It is the same fix under both candidates. **The fix did not work and the green run was luck.** It came back on `22a5b5a` with the same numbers, `{"floor":1057,"ratio":1,"inner":1056}`. One green run on an intermittent failure is not proof, and closing it on one was the mistake. Two claims made while closing it are also withdrawn: `hold_at_least` logging no refusal proved nothing, because the battery step does not forward the application's log at all (the `[INFO][sublore_lib::*]` lines in a CI run come from the other checks, never from inside `pnpm e2e`), so a Rust log line cannot answer anything here either. The instrument therefore lives in the page, where the check can read it: the spec now counts `resize` events over the ask. That separates the two remaining candidates, since the put-back and its retries all hang off that event. If the count is zero the retry never ran and the fix was inert by construction; if it is not, the ask reached `hold_at_least` and `inner_size()` answered with the width from before the resize.

  **Fourth run, and it changes the shape of the problem.** `fccb645` went green over the same check, so across four runs it is red, green, red, green. It is a coin flip, the diagnostic only prints on a failure, and the retry that was added on `e93c1cc` therefore moved the odds rather than fixing anything. That is exactly what a bounded retry would do here: it gives up after five asks fifty milliseconds apart, a quarter of a second, while `waitForWindowSize` allows five seconds. On a loaded runner a stale read outlasts the retry and nearly five seconds go unused.

  **The design the evidence points at, not yet built.** The decision is taken on the wrong side. The page calls only when `window.innerWidth < width`, so at the moment of the call the page already knows it is too narrow; `hold_at_least` then re-reads `inner_size()` and takes a second opinion that is the only value in the chain that can be stale. Removing that second opinion makes it deterministic rather than more probable: carry the page's own width across the boundary and let it decide, so `layout_set_minimum_width` sets the hint alone when the floor merely changed, and grows the window when the page says it is under the floor. `set_size` is then always a grow, because the page only asks when it is narrow. A `ResizeObserver` on the document element alongside the `resize` event is worth having with it, since the retry and the put-back both hang off one event today.

  **What proving it will take, given the base rate.** A single green run says nothing at fifty per cent. Either reproduce it here under load, which needs the X display and nothing else running on it, or run the same commit through CI enough times that a run of greens is not luck: four in a row is one chance in sixteen, six is one in sixty-four. Whichever is used, the number goes in this entry.

  **The loaded-runner theory is refuted, measured 2026-09-05.** `grid-columns.spec.js` was run five times on this machine pinned to two cores with `taskset -c 0-1`, which is harsher than a standard runner's four, and the floor check passed five times out of five. Starving the machine does not produce it, so whatever the runner does differently is not a matter of speed. What is left is the webview: this machine runs WebKitGTK 2.52.5 on Fedora 44 and the runner is Ubuntu 24.04, and when the `resize` event is dispatched and how fresh `inner_size()` is after it are that library's behaviour. **This defect is therefore not reproducible here at all, and no amount of local running will prove a fix.** The fix has to be deterministic by construction rather than by timing, which is what carrying the page's own width across the boundary buys, and the evidence has to be a run of green CI runs long enough that luck is ruled out at a fifty per cent base rate.

  **The second attempt, and what the fifth run finally showed.** The instrument answered on `a1e7c2f`: `{"floor":1057,"ratio":1,"resizes":1,"inner":1056,"outer":1056}`. The resize event fires, once, so the put-back ran, its guard was true and all five of its asks went out over 250 ms. The window did not move for the five seconds that followed, and a successful `set_size` would itself have produced a second resize event, so `resizes` staying at 1 says none of the five did anything. That leaves either `inner_size` answering stale for a quarter of a second across five calls, or `set_size` being called and ignored, and nothing on this machine can tell those apart.

  The fix therefore removes the branch rather than choosing between them. `layout_set_minimum_width` takes a `hold` flag and returns the width `inner_size` reported; the page sets `hold`, because the page is the side that has read itself narrower than the floor and that reading cannot be stale relative to its own decision, and with `hold` the window is grown without consulting the width the window reports. The returned width reaches the page as `data-floor-seen` and the check prints it, so if it fails again the two beliefs are both on the page and the remaining question is answered in one run rather than three.

  **The instrument answered, and it moved the defect out of the product.** With the branch gone the run printed `{"seen":"1056/1056","floor":1057,"resizes":1,"inner":1056,"outer":1056}`: `set_size(1057)` returns `Ok`, and the window reads 1056 immediately before it and 1056 immediately after. The call does nothing. So it was never a stale read, never the resize event, never the length of the retry: on that machine's GTK the application cannot resize its own window, and four reruns in a row failed the same way.

  **What actually keeps a user's window above the floor is the minimum size hint, and it is correct.** Measured here with `xprop`: the real toplevel carries `program specified minimum size: 706 by 600`, so a window manager honours it and a person cannot drag the window under its floor. Two wrong conclusions along the way are withdrawn rather than quietly dropped. First, "the hint never reaches X, so no window manager protects the floor" was read off the wrong window: an app under Xvfb has two windows named Sublore, a 10x10 decoy with no hints and the real 1024x700 toplevel, which is why the battery has a `findToplevel` of its own. Second, the run before that showed no hint and no call because the probe used the binary `cargo build` produces, which has no custom protocol and never loads the interface, which is the trap CONTRIBUTING already names for the battery.

  **So this is a check about the test environment, not about the product.** Under Xvfb there is no window manager, nothing enforces the hint, and the only thing left is the application resizing itself, which is best effort and differs by GTK version. The check asserts that fallback. The honest fix is to assert the mechanism that protects a person, which is the hint, and to say in the spec that nothing enforces it here. One thing has to be settled first: `interface-scale.spec.js:409` makes the same `settleAt(floor - 1, ..., floor)` call and passes on the same CI runs where this one fails, and until that difference is explained neither spec's version of the assertion is understood.

  **That difference is explained, and it names the mechanism.** The two call sites differ in where the window is standing when they ask. `interface-scale.spec.js` runs its size loop ending at `WIDE_WIDTH`, so it asks the shell to come back to the floor from a window the toolkit last knew as 1920 wide. `grid-columns.spec.js` asks from a window sitting on the floor itself: `pickSize(150)` raises the floor above the 1024 the run opens at, `hold_at_least` has just grown the window to exactly 1057, and only then does the check shrink it. The shrink comes from `xdotool`, so it changes what the server has and not what the toolkit believes, and the application then asks for 1057 from a toolkit that already believes it is 1057. Nothing is sent. From 1920 the same ask is a real change and is sent, which is why one spec passes and the other fails in the same run, and why neither reproduces on a newer GTK.

  So the fix is to say the true width before asking for the wanted one: `hold_at_least` now sets the window to the width `inner_size` just reported, when that differs from the floor, and then to the floor. The first call is a no-op in reality and a correction to the toolkit's bookkeeping; the second is then a change it will send. Both floor specs stay green here, where neither ever failed.

  **That was the fourth attempt and it failed like the other three: still `1056/1056`.** Two `set_size` calls in a row, the first naming the width the window has and the second the width it should have, and the window does not move. On that toolkit the application cannot resize its own window at all, and no amount of asking changes it.

  **So the assertion moved to the mechanism that actually protects a person, and the four attempts are why.** What keeps a window above the shell's floor on a desktop is the minimum size the window declares, and that is correct and was never in doubt once it was read off the right window: `program specified minimum size: 706 by 600`. A window manager honours it. This battery runs on a bare X server with no window manager, so nothing there enforces it, and the shell asking for the width back is a best effort that one toolkit version performs and another drops without a word. Asserting that best effort was asserting something the product does not promise. `e2e/lib/x11.js` gains `minimumWidthHint`, and the check now asserts the declared minimum equals the shell's own floor, which no check covered before. Proved able to fail: with `set_min_size` given `width - 50`, the check goes red and names the declared minimum.

  **What is left, and it is a real question rather than a leftover.** `interface-scale.spec.js:409` still asserts the best effort and still passes, because it asks from a window the test had just made wide. It is passing by an accident of ordering rather than because the shell is holding anything, and it should be moved to the same assertion. Left as its own change so this one stays about the grid.

- [x] **N28 A seek's canvas read could be one paint behind, and the arithmetic of two runs proves it (filed 2026-09-05, closed 2026-09-05).** `seekTo` waited for the transport slider to read the target and then read the canvas, on the claim that a slider reading the target is the render the canvas was painted in. That claim is false: the slider and the canvas are painted by different effects. Two failures, one cause. On `1ec009e` the stale read poisoned the calibration itself, and the size of the error is its signature: a first sample taken at 0 s instead of 5 s makes the computed scale exactly 0.8 of the true one and the origin `5000 + 0.8f`, so 40 s is drawn as `5000 + 0.8 x 40000 = 37000`, three seconds out. CI measured 2982 ms, and the scale it reported, 85.8 ms per column, is 0.8 of the 107.5 measured later. On `529036f` the same lag hit the second test instead: the head read 233 columns, and from that run's own diagnostic (`drawn 20161 ms, column 188 of 107.5 ms each`, so the origin is -49 ms) column 233 is `(25000 + 49) / 107.5`, exactly the 25 second calibration seek still on screen. The fix waits for the head to be redrawn in a different column, which is the repaint itself rather than a stopwatch.
- [x] **N27 A switch to the audio track that is already playing sometimes starts no job, and nothing says why (filed 2026-09-05, closed 2026-09-09).** `waveform-tracks.spec.js`'s cache check timed a switch to the second track while the check before it had already left that track current, so it was a click on the track that is already playing. On the CI run of `8b14285` the app's own log shows job 4 finishing at 01:40:28 and the next job at 01:40:58, thirty seconds later, which is the check's timeout: in between, the click landed and no job started. The check has been changed to put the app on the first track before it times anything, so it no longer measures a switch that is not one, and it passes on Linux.
  - **Two hypotheses were killed by reading, and both are worth writing down because they are the obvious ones.** The menu item is not greyed for the current track: `enabled` is `audio.tracks.length > 1` (`src/App.tsx:934`), so both items are live whichever is playing. And the waveform is not blanked by a refused switch: the peaks are cleared only on `audio://started` (`src/hooks/useAudioPeaks.ts:78`), which a job that never starts never emits.
  - **The condition is now named, by reading `AudioState::begin`.** It refuses only while the slot still holds a job with the same target that has not been cancelled, and `end` frees that slot from the job's own blocking task, after the job has already written its last line. So a switch back to the stream that just finished lands in the window between "job finished" in the log and the slot being given back, and is refused with `Busy`. That is consistent with job 4 succeeding as a second start moments earlier: job 3 was superseded rather than complete, which is the other branch.
  - **The line that was missing now exists.** `audio_switch_track` writes one warn line naming the track and the refusal's own sentence when `start_job` refuses, so the next occurrence says what it was instead of leaving a thirty second hole. Whether that window is the whole of the failure is still not proved: what is proved is that it can no longer happen silently.
  - **Read again 2026-09-09: the frontend believes a surface it does not get.** The `catch` in `useAudioTracks.ts:73` carries the comment "the waveform's own failure line covers a switch that could not be peaked", and for a `Busy` refusal that is not true: `begin` refuses before any job exists, so no `audio://failed` is emitted and the waveform says nothing. That is the whole of why it is silent, and it is a wrong belief written down rather than a missing line.
  - **What closing it needs, and why it has not been done here.** Either the refusal reaches the status bar's timed slot, which is small, or the frontend never asks for the track it is already on, which is smaller but wrong for a retry after a failed peak, where `currentId` names a track whose job did not finish. The first is the right one. What has held it back is the check: forcing a `Busy` means racing a switch against a job that is finishing, and this backlog already carries eight entries about checks that turn on timing. A flaky check bought for a fault that is not a data risk and not visible as a fault is a bad trade, so this is named and left rather than half done.
  - **What the user would see, if it is what it looks like.** Clicking the audio track already playing does nothing at all, silently. That is not a data risk and it is not visible as a fault, which is why it went unnoticed.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux, on the half that was the fault.** The title says nothing says why, and that is what is fixed: a refused switch reaches the status bar's timed slot, and the comment that claimed the waveform covered the case now says which half it covers and which it does not. A successful switch stays silent, which the whole battery still proves. **It ships without a check, and the reason is written rather than left out.** Forcing a refusal through the interface is not possible deterministically: the menu lists real tracks and greys when there is nothing to switch to, and calling the command directly goes around the hook, so it would prove the backend refuses and not that the panel says so. The remaining routes are a race, which is the ninth timing-dependent check this backlog does not need, a test-only door into the product, or a frontend unit runner this repository does not have.
- [x] **N24 The E2E battery is serial for a reason that can be removed, and it costs six and a half minutes a gate (filed 2026-09-04, closed 2026-09-09 at two workers).** `wdio.conf.js` sets `maxInstances: 1`, so twenty-seven spec files run one after another. The owner freed the machine for heavy work on 2026-09-04, so the ceiling is no longer thermal: it is that every worker would share one `SUBLORE_E2E_DATA_HOME`, and the app reopens whichever project it last had, which is N19's mechanism turned into a collision between live sessions rather than between neighbours in time.
  - **What it needs:** a data home per worker rather than per run. `wdio.conf.js:19` makes one in the launcher and every worker inherits it; a `beforeSession` could give each its own directory under it. The stub sidecar and the model directory are read only and stay shared, so only the project state has to split.
  - **What it would buy:** the gate is the loop's slowest step by a wide margin, and three or four workers would take it under two minutes. Every block in this session paid it at least twice, once for the mutations and once for the final run.
  - **Looked at properly on 2026-09-07, and it is harder than this entry says.** Two things were found. The first is mechanical: a data home per worker cannot be handed to the app through the session, because tauri-driver's `tauri:options` carries two fields, `application` and `args`, and no environment. The app inherits the driver's own environment, so a data home per worker means a **driver per worker**, which means a port per worker, and the tally the count guard reads is keyed on that port and would split four ways. It also means a **display** per worker, because `findToplevel` picks the app by its geometry and four apps on one display are four windows that match. All of that is buildable; none of it is the line this entry implies.
  - **The second is the reason to stop and think.** Trap 5 in this file says two batteries at once falsify every timed check, and the checks that count frames and events are exactly the ones N40 has just been about: one of them failed on this machine in a full run on 2026-09-07 and passed alone straight afterwards. Four workers put the same load on the machine that two batteries do. So N24 would multiply the failure N40 describes, and it should follow the timed checks being made to measure the thing rather than the machine, not lead it.
  - **Measured 2026-09-09: the suite is order independent.** Every one of the 72 spec files was run on its own, one after another, and all 72 passed. Before the data home split two did not: `playhead.spec.js` was red alone even on main, and `use-video-track.spec.js` was reading a cache an earlier spec had warmed. So the objection this entry opens with, that workers would collide through shared state, is now answered by measurement rather than by argument. What is left is the load question below, which is about timing and not about state.
  - **The data home half is done, 2026-09-09.** N19 closed: each spec already gets a directory of its own, made in `beforeSession` before the driver starts, and the shared read-only artefacts are anchored to the run. So the collision this entry names between live sessions is no longer the obstacle. What is still true is the second reason written below, the timed checks, and the GTK question under it.
  - **What to watch:** the machine stops initialising GTK after about four `xvfb-run` launches in quick succession, measured on 2026-09-03 under the old thermal ceiling. Whether that was resource exhaustion that a healthy machine survives is unknown and is the first thing to measure, because it is exactly what parallel workers would do.
  - Not attempted yet. Filed rather than half done, and it is infrastructure rather than product, so it waits behind the feature it would speed up.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux: 19:00 in serial, 9:55 on two workers, 72 spec files and 428 tests green either way.** Each worker gets a driver, a port derived from its own number, and an Xvfb of its own, because `findToplevel` picks the app by geometry and two apps on one display are two windows that match. **No check that measures time reddened**, which is the evidence this entry has wanted since it was written. The tally splitting was predicted here and happened; what was not predicted is that the first fix for it did not work, and the first parallel run said so by reporting four spec files passed and zero tests: a module body runs before the body of the file importing it, so the launcher built the tally's key before setting the variable it is made from while the workers inherited it already set. Read when used now. **Two is what is proved and two is what ships.** Raising it is one line and another measurement, and the GTK question below is the reason it should stay a measurement.

- [x] **N8e The rest of the host table.** Closed 2026-09-05: every slot the host builds is filled, verified by reading the construction site. Was: four of the seventeen slots are filled. `document`, `cue_at`, `log` and `find` work; `propose`, `for_each_line`, `db_run`, `db_transaction`, the three panel calls, `status`, `progress`, `should_cancel` and the wiring that makes `invoke` reach a module are not built. Criteria are in the planning archive as `docs/module-host-tasks.md`, H5 to H8.

  - **The session lock is held across the whole module call, and that is memory safety rather than economy.** `cue_at` hands over text borrowed out of the session, and the thing that can drop it is not the module: it is the user, committing an edit from the window's own thread while the call is in flight. The cost of the alternative was measured first and was irrelevant, which is what the measurement was for: 3.9 ns per uncontended lock, 7.84 microseconds over two thousand cues.
  - **Section 2.5 asks for a generation number that cannot be a check.** A generation only checks anything if the caller presents one, and a module holds the host pointer and no token. Both things it was written for are caught without it.
  - **A prediction about normalized form was wrong.** An ASS line break arrives as the two characters `\N`, not as a newline: normalization collapses `\r\n` and nothing else. The check that found it was also proving the wrong thing, asserting the CRLF rule on an ASS fixture where a carriage return inside a cue cannot exist.
  - **Clippy caught an aliasing hazard a written safety argument had waved at.** Handing the session out as `&mut` from a `&self` can be done twice, and a comment saying no callback keeps it is not a constraint. The accessor returns the raw pointer now and each reader makes its own shared reference at the point of use.
  - **The closed module loads through this, for real.** A cdylib built in a separate private repository, linking only `sublore-module-api`, was put beside the binary: the app created it, the module called `log` and `find` through the table, the core's comparison answered, and it contributed four items.

Nine behavioural checks against a real migrated database, plus two unit checks on the id and the prefix. Both mutations the criteria named turned exactly the right checks red.

The indexes are stable only because of the 2026-09-03 ruling that commands grey and never vanish, and the comment says so.

The next step is to find what has focus after Add episode is confirmed, not to change another constant.

The 346x166 window in the failure's window list is therefore not what is in the way.

With a project open, Create project is greyed (`ProjectRail.tsx:95`), so the menu opens on index 1 and two Downs land on index 3, **Close project**. The 2026-09-03 ruling fixes the item list; it says nothing about where the cursor starts, and that is the half the comment assumed.
  - **The assertion behind it could not tell, and that is the worse half.** The episode was proved by the size and mtime of `project.sublore-wal` changing. A clean close of a WAL database checkpoints and removes that file, which is a change, so the check printed `ok` on a project it had just closed. Every walk after that ran against an empty rail. This is `e2e/README.md`'s own rule about a green run that proves nothing, met in the field.
  - **The 346x166 window was not in the way either.** It is the native video surface, defined in `video-empty.spec.js:52-58` as the toplevel's one child over 50x50, unmapped until a video is ready and receiving no events. No probe was needed to identify it, only reading.

The check asserts on those lines instead of on side effects, so a walk that presses the wrong item says which one it pressed. The Down count is now `(item - from + items) % items` with the cursor's start stated, and every gesture is followed by an assertion naming the command that actually ran.
