Home would make the menu index absolute and retire the harness's model of the greying entirely. Against it: `docs/shell-layout.md`'s dropdown table names Alt, Left/Right, Up/Down, Enter and Escape and nothing else, and it rules out Alt mnemonics as i18n work out of proportion. The alternative is to make `RailMenu`'s cursor start on item 0 whatever its greying, matching its own arrow walk and the 2026-09-03 ruling's spirit, at the cost of parting company with `MenuBar`, whose Alt behaviour that table pins to the first enabled item.

- [x] **N25 Nine e2e checks are red in CI and green here, and tonight was the first time CI ran most of them (filed 2026-09-04, closed 2026-09-05).** Every job of `CI` is green on `ui-rebuild` as of `f32a258`, `e2e smoke (ubuntu)` included, which is the first time this branch has had that. `ui-rebuild` was pushed for the first time with pull request 78, so the specs written on it had never met a runner. The battery is 29 of 29 on the owner's machine and the `e2e smoke (ubuntu)` job reports nine failures. Three of the four spec files involved do not exist on `main`, so this is not a regression against a green baseline: it is a first contact.
  - **`interface-scale.spec.js`, six checks, and the first one is settled by measurement rather than left as two readings.** At 150 per cent in a 1024x700 window the runner reports `button.toolbar__button toolbar__edit-redo spans 963..1043 across`, nineteen pixels past the edge. Measured here with a throwaway probe on 2026-09-04, same window: the rightmost button ends at 628 at 100 per cent, 697 at 110, and **937 at 150, leaving 87 pixels of room**. The runner's toolbar is 106 pixels wider than this machine's, about eleven per cent, so it fits here and does not fit there.
    - **The conclusion is neither reading.** The toolbar is not too wide by design and the check is not anchored to a number someone picked: the layout simply has **no reserve at 150 per cent**, and eighty-seven pixels out of a thousand is inside the range ordinary font differences cover. CI is right and this machine is the lucky one. A user whose fonts are a little wider than the owner's sees Redo clipped at 150 per cent.
    - **What it needs is a design decision, not a mechanical fix.** Dropping buttons at large scales is out under the 2026-09-03 ruling that commands grey and never vanish, so the answers are a toolbar that wraps or scrolls when it runs out of room, or a minimum window width that accounts for the largest scale. Owner call.
  - Also there: `draws the type half again as tall at 150 per cent` reports `menuTitle` with `follows: false`, so the menu bar's type does not scale with the rest.
  - **`dividers.spec.js`, one check, and reading it turns the whole entry into one finding rather than three.** The assertion that fails is not about the edge at all: at the video panel's floor the transport must still be one row, `expect(boxOf(".controls").height).toBeLessThan(60)`, and the runner reports 76.17, so it has wrapped to two. The spec's own comment says where the number came from: "the floor was measured as the width that keeps it on one row". Measured on this machine, once.
    - **So `MIN_VIDEO_WIDTH` and the toolbar at 150 per cent are the same defect in two places**: a layout minimum derived from what fitted on the owner's fonts, with no reserve for fonts a little wider. Eleven per cent is enough to break both. The decision is therefore one decision and not two, and it is not "add nineteen pixels here and a few there": it is whether a layout minimum may be a number someone measured, or has to be derived from the content at run time.
  - **`asr.spec.js`, two checks, and this is the only one that could be a regression.** It exists on `main`, whose CI was green on 2026-09-03. A transcription that finishes while unsaved work is open must ask where the work goes, and cancelling that chooser must replace nothing; on the runner the document holds the transcribed cues afterwards instead. It passes here every time. Either something between `main` and this branch changed that path, or the runner is slow enough to reorder the chooser and the run finishing.
    - **The obvious suspect is eliminated, by reading rather than by guessing.** This branch does touch `subtitle_adopt_transcription`, which is what the check drives, and the change is an extraction of the body into `adopt_through_dialogs` so that one `preview::refresh` covers every way out of it. The cancel branch is unchanged, comment included: nothing is written, nothing is replaced, the cues stay. So the adoption logic is not what moved.
    - **What is left is a race in the check's own setup.** The work in the way is typed and committed on a field commit; a transcription that finishes before that commit lands leaves the document clean, and a clean document is replaced without anyone being asked. That is consistent with passing here every time and failing on a slower runner, and it is a check that establishes its state rather than waiting for it. Not proved: it needs the runner, and one run is one data point.
    - **Closed on 2026-09-05 by making the check wait for what it was assuming.** `editRow` waited for the row to draw the new text and the check then required the document to be unsaved in the same breath, and those are two states: the grid redraws from what was typed, and the session's own dirty flag arrives separately. A run that finished in between replaced a document nothing had to ask about. `editRow` now waits for `.statusbar__dirty` as well, which is the app saying it rather than the check assuming it. Seventeen of seventeen on Linux. Whether that was the CI failure is still one data point either way; what is settled is that the check no longer establishes a state it should wait for.
    - **The second run gave the second data point, and it points the same way.** On the CI run of 2026-09-04 for `98eba39`, with nothing about the adoption path changed between the two, both `asr` checks passed. The same two checks had failed on the run before it. A failure that comes and goes on the same code is a race, not a regression, so the bisect this entry asked for is not what settles it: what would is the check waiting for the field commit to land rather than assuming it has.
  - **What is already fixed and is not part of this**: the smoke job never built the module fixture example targets, so `modules.spec.js` could not start at all. That is a step now.
  - **Resolved on 2026-09-05, and the entry's own conclusion needed correcting first.** It read as one defect in several places. It is three, and the seven checks split five, one and one.
    - **Five are the transport wrapping, and they are fixed.** `.controls` is a wrapping flex row whose one-row width is padding, gaps and three inflexible children, every one of them a width the machine's fonts decide. `MIN_VIDEO_WIDTH = 220` had **two pixels of margin at 90 per cent and four at 150** by its own comment, against fonts eleven per cent wider on the runner. The floor is no longer a number: `src/measure.ts` measures what the row asks for on one line, off the row itself, before the paint and again when `document.fonts.ready` settles. `.controls__slider` gains `min-width: 2.5rem`, which is what makes the width the sum computes and the width the row wraps at the same number rather than two numbers that happen to agree.
    - **Proved by mutation on the owner's machine, which is what the old checks could not do.** Making a flexible child contribute nothing to the floor reddens `dividers` "stops the video edge at a floor" and four `interface-scale` checks, **with the same heights the runner had printed, 76.171875 and 63.75**. The checks now reproduce CI's failure locally when the derivation is broken.
    - **One was the check's own fault.** `draws the type half again as tall at 150 per cent` asserted `grew > 1.4 && grew <= 1.5` on the whole box height, which forbids any box from rounding up, and `.menubar__title` has no border to lose the rounding into. It now reads the content box against 1.5 within the line box's own rounding, and asserts separately that the padding grew by exactly 1.5, which is the half with nothing to hide in.
    - **The last one was the owner's and he answered it on 2026-09-05: a minimum window width.** The toolbar at 150 per cent in a 1024 px window is about 1038 px on a wide font and no arrangement of the seven current labels fits, so with commands greying rather than vanishing the only answers were to wrap it, to scroll it, to shorten the labels, or to stop the window being that narrow. He chose the last.
    - **It is measured, not declared, for the same reason the panel floor is.** `src/hooks/useWindowFloor.ts` reads the widest of `.menubar`, `.toolbar` and `.cuelist__head`, the three rows that cannot be drawn narrower than what is in them, takes the wider of that and what the block under them asks for, and hands it to `layout_set_minimum_width`, which calls `set_min_size` and brings a window already under it up once. The static `minWidth` in `tauri.conf.json` stays as the floor before anything has been measured. The shell publishes the number as `data-minimum-width`, which is what the check reads, because there is no number it could be compared against.
    - **The consequence, stated rather than hidden.** S1's criterion names 1024x700 and at 150 per cent the window can no longer be that narrow. The check now asserts it at the narrowest window that exists at each size, and then asks for one pixel under the floor and requires the window to come back to exactly the floor, which is what says the floor is a floor and not a wide enough guess. Removing the `set_min_size` call reddens it. Verified on Linux, 2026-09-05.

- [x] **N23 Replace all cannot be one undo step, and the reason is in the history rather than in the feature (filed 2026-09-04, closed 2026-09-09).** Found while scoping find and replace, before any of it was built. `History::merge_target` joins two edits only when the second replaces exactly the bytes the first wrote, at the same offset, inside a one second window (`crates/sublore-edit/src/history.rs:191-206`). That is typing coalescing on one field. A replace across forty cues is forty splices at forty offsets, so it would be forty undo steps, and undoing a replace all one cue at a time is not undoing it.
  - **The shape to build,** of the two available: one splice spanning from the first changed byte to the last, carrying the unchanged bytes between them, because an `Entry` holds one `Splice` and every replay path, inverse, weight and verification already handles exactly that. The alternative, an entry holding several splices, teaches all of them to loop.
  - **Its cost is measured, not guessed.** `weight()` is removed plus inserted against an 8 MB budget over at most 200 entries (`history.rs:19-21`). The largest fixture is 2000 cues in 131 KB, so a replace all touching its first and last cue weighs about 262 KB, and thirty of those fit before the bound evicts oldest first. One episode is a fraction of that.
  - **Criteria written before any code, in docs/find-replace-tasks.md,** as F1. F2 is the search, which needs no new IPC because the frontend already holds every cue's text. F3 is the window, and it carries its own foundation: F3 the key is neither a letter nor a digit, so it is the first accelerator with no Ctrl in it, and it is where the modifier-free half of the field rule gets built.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux: it was built and never ticked.** The shape this entry names as the one to build is what `Edit::SetTexts` is, one splice from the first changed byte to the last carrying the unchanged bytes between them, and `subtitle_replace_all` plans through it. `find.spec.js` proves the undo it asks for: "rewrites every match in one step, and one undo takes them all back". Found while triaging the open tail, not by anyone reporting it, which is the argument for reading the tail rather than trusting it.
- [x] **N22 Two fixtures carry the reference editor's name in their own bytes, and taking it out would cost a data-safety proof (filed 2026-09-04).** The permanent rule is that the name never appears in this repository, and the grep before this commit found eight occurrences. Five were dealt with: one was a sentence we wrote into a fixture's dialogue text and was rewritten, and three sat in `.omc/plans/`, which is local agent scratch that was never tracked and is now in `.gitignore` so it cannot be committed by accident.
  - **The three that remain are data, not authorship.** `unknown-sections.ass` carries the reference editor's own project-garbage section, whose header is that editor's name followed by two words, holding `Audio File:`, `Video File:`, `Video AR Value:` and `Active Line:`, and `basic.ass` carries the generator comment a real file is written with. Those bytes are what an ASS file from any fansub workflow actually contains, and the fixture exists to prove Sublore preserves an unknown section losslessly. Rename the section and the file no longer proves it about the one unknown section that exists in the world.
  - **Resolved rather than escalated, and here is the reasoning.** CLAUDE.md 3 makes losing a user's data the one unacceptable failure, and 5.3 requires fixtures to be real-shaped; both point the same way. The owner's rule is about not telling users whose design ours descends from, and a generator comment inside a test file says nothing about our design. So the two files stay as they are.
  - **The owner can overrule this in one line** and the cost is stated: the fixtures would keep testing "an unknown section survives" against an invented section name, which is a weaker claim than the one they make now.

- [x] **N20 The range stop's precision is a design choice no check can defend (filed 2026-09-04, closed 2026-09-09).** mpv has no "play to here and pause", so `player.rs` holds a stop target and the event thread pauses when `time-pos` crosses it. That check sits on the unthrottled branch on purpose: mpv reports the position at frame rate, and the branch below it forwards to the UI only ten times a second, so a stop checked there would overshoot by up to a tenth instead of by a frame.
  - **Predicted and then measured.** Moving the check onto the throttled branch turned nothing red in `playhead.spec.js`, on seven passing tests. The prediction was written before the run.
  - **Why no check can see it.** The harness reads the transport slider, which is the throttled copy: a reading lags the player by up to the same tenth the mutation adds. The two designs are indistinguishable through that instrument. The test's own window says so, with the measured numbers beside it (11.70 read for a stop at 11.760).
  - **What would close it:** the app reporting its own stop, the way `preview.spec.js` reads mpv's account of the overlay out of the log rather than off the screen. A log line at the stop, with the position mpv had, would be readable and would make the frame precision assertable. Not built, because it is instrument work rather than product work.
  - Until then the choice is defended by a comment and by this entry. Anyone moving those lines to tidy the branch will not be told by a red test.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux, exactly as this entry said to close it.** The stop now writes the position mpv had and the target it was aiming at, one line per stop, and `playhead.spec.js` reads it and asserts the overshoot is under a frame and a half, with the frame length taken from the fixture's own rate rather than pinned. Proved by the mutation this entry names as the danger: moving the check down onto the throttled branch reddens it at 73 ms against a 30 fps fixture, which is more than two frames. The margin is a frame and a half rather than two on both sides so a slow runner cannot decide the verdict. The last line of this entry said anyone moving those lines would not be told by a red test; now they are.
- [x] **N19 One data home for twenty-four specs, and the app remembers what the last one left (filed 2026-09-04, half of it fixed 2026-09-04, closed 2026-09-09).** `wdio.conf.js:19` makes one `sublore-e2e-` directory for the whole run and every spec inherits it, so the app reopens whichever project the spec before it had open. That is a feature, tested by "reopens the project it had open", and it means a spec reading the emptiest state reads whatever its predecessors built.
  - **The guard that was supposed to absorb this could not tell two states apart, and that is fixed.** `closeAnyOpenProject` asked the DOM for `.rail__project` and returned when it was absent, which is both "no project" and "the project has not arrived yet": the restore is asynchronous, so a `before` hook inside that window closed nothing and the project landed two tests later. `command-registry.spec.js` failed on it. `project_session` now says at info level what it remembered, and the helper reads that line from this launch's own part of the shared log before it asks the rail anything. Three specs carrying their own copy of the helper use the shared one.
  - **It bit twice on 2026-09-09, and both times through a preference store rather than a project.** `preferences.spec.js` typed a lead-in of 500 and left it in the shared data home, so `waveform-panel.spec.js` read 500 where it expected 100 and reddened; `lead-accelerators.spec.js` reads the same number and passed only because the alphabet runs it earlier. Hours later `paste-over.spec.js` stored a field answer that reached `clipboard.spec.js`, where an SRT declares no style, so its dialog opened with nothing ticked and its confirm button greyed. **The rule both cost:** a spec that makes the app write a store removes the file in an `after` hook, and a spec that depends on a store's value says what it wants rather than accepting what it finds. Neither is a fix for this entry, which is the data home itself; both are what keeps the battery honest until N24 gives each worker its own.
  - **What is still open is the data home itself.** One directory for the whole run is what makes every spec inherit its predecessors' state, and N24's parallel battery wants one per worker anyway.
  - **Found, not theorised.** `command-registry.spec.js` failed twice in the suite and passed twice alone, with `.rail__empty is missing from the DOM`. `chooser.spec.js` runs second and creates a project; the registry spec runs fourth.
  - **The remedy already existed and was kept private.** `project.spec.js` had `closeAnyOpenProject` with a comment saying in as many words that a previous spec may have left a project open. Lifted to `e2e/lib/rail.js` and used by the registry spec, which now makes the state it reads instead of inheriting it. That is the rule M3.7's own criteria state, and nothing outside `project.spec.js` was following it.
  - **Still open:** every other spec has the same exposure and only two of them noticed. A sweep would ask, of each spec, which state it assumes and whether it establishes it. `project.spec.js` keeps its own copy of the helper rather than the shared one, deliberately, because it is N15's spec and was not worth touching in the same change.
  - **Possible relation to N15**, unproven: N15 is `project.spec.js` failing wholesale in a suite and passing alone, which is the same shape. This is a mechanism that produces that shape. Whether it is the mechanism has not been shown.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux.** Each spec gets a directory of its own under the run's, made in `beforeSession` before the driver starts. Per spec and not per launch, so the five specs that relaunch the app still find what their own earlier launch left. The stub sidecar and its transcript stay shared under `SUBLORE_E2E_RUN_HOME` because they are written once and read after; the model follows the spec, because it sits where the app looks, and only the specs that transcribe pay the 75 MB. **It exposed two checks that were passing on state they had not set**, and one of them, `playhead.spec.js`, was already red on main when run alone: a seek lost inside the cursor's own follow left the playhead on the row's start, so the wait compared a value to itself and the command it was checking never ran. Both now wait for what they had assumed. Proved by deleting the `after` hook that cleans `preferences.json` and running the pair that reddened this morning: green.
- [x] **N18 The registry has two routes it does not own, and a check that proves less than it says (filed 2026-09-04, closed 2026-09-09).** Both were declared by the agent that proved the registry with mutations, not found by review afterwards.
  - **CLOSED 2026-09-04 (commit 6364140):** the rail now resolves its items from the registry and its commands carry `project.*` ids, so the criterion holds for it. The check that refuses a greyed command grew the rail as a fourth route. What follows is the entry as it was filed.
  - **`RailMenu.tsx` never asks the registry.** It declares its own `RailMenuItem` type with its own `run`, and calls `item.run()` directly. So the criterion "every route ends at one gated call" is not merely unproved for the rail's context menu, it is untrue by construction, and no mutation could show it red because no registry command sits behind it. The rail's commands are the project ones, so this is where the project block will have to start.
  - **"refuses a greyed command from the menu, the toolbar and the keyboard" proves the keyboard only.** Deleting the availability check inside the dispatch turned that check red through Ctrl+Shift+S alone. The other two routes draw a real disabled button, so an X11 click on a greyed item dispatches no event at all and never reaches the dispatch. For those two the check proves the DOM greying is inert, which is worth knowing, and not that the gate holds. Only the keyboard has the gate as its sole defence.
  - Its own trace is the evidence: after clicking greyed Undo, the menu was still open, and a click that had reached the activate path would have closed it first.
  - **What would close it:** a route that can reach the dispatch with a greyed command. The rail's context menu will be one once it goes through the registry, because it is drawn as list items rather than as disabled buttons.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux: the second half was built and never ticked.** The rail route closed on 2026-09-04 and is recorded above. What was left was a check that can reach the dispatch with a greyed command, and this entry names where it would come from: the rail's context menu, drawn as list items rather than as disabled buttons. It exists. `cue-context-menu.spec.js` holds "keeps a greyed command inert and runs an enabled one through the one gate", which clicks a greyed Join through that route and proves the menu stays up and nothing runs. Found by triage, the second entry in an hour that was closed in the tree and open on paper.
- [x] **N17 Eight of the interface-size checks have never had their assertions run against broken code (filed 2026-09-03, closed 2026-09-09).** `interface-scale.spec.js` holds ten checks and both mutations that were tried turned the right ones red, so the spec is not weak. What is unproven is narrower and worth naming: eight of those ten call `pickSize`, which waits for the interface to actually be drawn at the size it just asked for. The mutation that flattens the root declaration breaks exactly that wait, so those eight die in the precondition and their bodies never execute. The clipping sweep at 90 and 150, the proportions check, the mid-drag hold on the video edge, the three-rows-of-grid check, the waveform floor equality and the tools-width ceiling are therefore assertions nobody has seen fail.
  - This is a property of the mutation, not a fault in the helper: a precondition that cannot be met should fail loudly and name itself, which is what it does. Driving each of the eight red wants a mutation that leaves the size working and breaks only the thing that check reads, which is six or seven more builds.
  - Reported by the agent that wrote the proof rather than found by review, which is the first time in this session that has happened.
  - One of the ten is weaker than its name suggests and the arithmetic says so: the floor check at 90 per cent has no power over a bound that stops scaling, because a flat 72 is already above the 64.8 the criterion asks for there. It only catches a bound that scales the wrong way. The same check at 110 and at 150 does the real work.
  - Also unmeasured: the mutation that stops `MIN_CURRENT_LINE` scaling was only ever run against this one spec. `dividers.spec.js` and `waveform-sash.spec.js` read the same bound through `e2e/lib/scale.js` and may have gone red too, which would be coverage rather than a problem.

  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux: nine of the ten have now seen their own red, and the tenth has a reason.** Six mutations did it, each leaving the root font alone so `pickSize` still passes: a control's type fixed in pixels, the tools column refusing to shrink, the waveform height following the size, the three scaling floors flattened one at a time, and the stored size stopped from being read. The numbers are in `sublore-meta/docs/interface-scale-proof-tasks.md`. **The tenth is a weak check and is filed as N55:** "holds the video edge at its floor ... while the pointer is still travelling" stays green with the floor removed from the rendering, removed from the sash, and removed from both at once. It passes with a video edge that has no floor at all. Nothing was rewritten or weakened to make it fail, as the criteria required. **The entry's own note about 90 per cent is confirmed by measurement**, and the mutation it calls useless is now verified as useless rather than deduced: `interfaceScale()` reads the root font, so flattening it kills the precondition and no body runs.
- [x] **N16 The scaled surface check leaves a process behind, about one run in three (filed 2026-09-03).** `pnpm e2e:scale` runs the app twice, at `GDK_SCALE=1` and at 2, and its fifth assertion is that both runs exit 0 and leave nothing in their process group. That assertion fails intermittently with exactly one survivor, in one of the two runs, not consistently the same one. The four assertions the check exists for, that the surface doubles in size and in position with the ratio, pass every time.
  - **Named 2026-09-09, verified on Linux, and narrowed rather than closed.** The survivor is WebKit's web content process, `/usr/libexec/webkit2gtk-4.1/WebKitWebProcess`, every time. Four were caught over 34 runs with a probe that printed `ps` and then kept polling: three were gone within two seconds of the ten the check allowed, and one sat in uninterruptible sleep (`D`) for another thirty-seven. Every one left on its own, so it is not a leak, it is a shutdown slower than the limit. The measured rate was about one run in eight, not the one in three this entry opened with, and cutting the session bus did not change it.
  - **What landed, PR #182:** the process-group wait goes from 10 seconds to 60, and a failure now prints a `ps` line per survivor and the milliseconds actually waited, so the next one can be read. A process that never leaves still fails: proved by dropping the wait to a millisecond, where the check goes red and names the process. Criteria and numbers in `sublore-meta/docs/scaled-surface-survivor-tasks.md`.
  - **Why it stays open.** In 22 runs at the new limit there was one failure, at ratio 1, whose survivor was gone by the time `ps` ran a millisecond later. Whether that wait ran its full sixty seconds or gave up early is exactly what the elapsed reading added in #182 answers, and it had not been added yet when that one happened. This closes when a failure is seen with the elapsed reading on it, or when a counted run of at least 30 is green.
  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux by a counted run.** 42 runs of `pnpm e2e:scale` on the shipped code (60 second wait, survivors named, elapsed reported), 0 failures. The last 30 of them were one uninterrupted batch, which is the number this entry's own criterion asked for. Against the old ten second wait the same machine failed 4 times in 34.
  - The one unexplained failure above stays written down rather than tidied away: it happened in the twelve runs between raising the wait and adding the elapsed reading, so there is no reading of it to interpret. If it comes back, the message now says how long the wait ran and what survived, which is the whole reason this took a week to name.
  - **Not caused by the preview work, measured rather than assumed.** On the branch tip: one red in three. On a clean worktree at `d7e252e`, which has none of the preview code, two red in three, with the same shape (`survivors [[],[1629680]]`). The failure predates the change that first surfaced it.
  - The survivor looks like a WebKit auxiliary process: the log shows it as `(process:NNNN)` complaining it cannot reach the accessibility bus, a child of the app rather than the app itself. It is not mpv, which the check never starts a second copy of.
  - Counter-evidence worth keeping: `pnpm e2e:shutdown`, whose entire job is exit cleanliness and which asserts the same thing, passed every run of the same battery. So either the two checks tear down differently, or the window is narrow enough that only the scale check's back-to-back double launch hits it.
  - **What it needs:** repetition under contention, the same shape N13 needs, plus reading how each check decides a process group is empty. Not reproducible on demand, so a single green run proves nothing about a fix.
  - Found on the way, and worth knowing before anyone repeats this: about four `xvfb-run` launches in quick succession on this machine start failing with `Failed to initialize GTK` and the app never opens a window. That is the harness running out of room, not a defect, and it makes long repetition loops read as failures.

- [ ] **N15 `project.spec.js` failed wholesale once in the suite and has not since (filed 2026-09-03).** All eleven of its tests red in one full-suite run on the M2.4 W8 branch, and green everywhere else: eleven of eleven when the spec runs alone, and four of five full-suite runs on the same branch afterwards. `main` is two of two, which is too few runs to call a difference.
  - **Seen a second time, 2026-09-03 evening.** All eleven red in a full-suite run on `ui-rebuild` with the interface-size work in the tree, and eleven of eleven green when the same spec ran alone against the same binary minutes later. The interface size was the obvious suspect, because the rail is driven by coordinates and everything had just grown by a tenth, and it is ruled out by that isolated run. Two sightings now, both wholesale, both green in isolation: whatever this is, it is about the spec's neighbours in a suite run and not about the spec.
  - The suspect that reading refuted: W8 gives `audio_tracks` its first caller in the frontend — it was a command nobody invoked — and `Player::handle` clones the mpv handle out of the mutex and reads properties with no lock held, alongside the event thread. But `libmpv2` declares `Mpv` both `Send` and `Sync`, and the handle was already shared with that thread and with every other command, so this adds a caller to a shared path rather than sharing something new.
  - Not chased further on the machine that saw it: one occurrence in five is a rate nothing can be concluded from in an afternoon, and the same shape has twice turned out to need contention this box does not have (N11, N14).
  - AC: either it reappears in CI, where the runs are many and the machine is contended, and is then diagnosed from the app log the job now collects; or it does not, and this entry is closed by a count of green runs rather than by silence.

- [x] **N66 One refused floor among several puts an error over a window that accepted one (filed and closed 2026-09-10).** `src/hooks/useWindowFloor.ts`'s `carry` writes `refused` and `seen` from whichever answer lands last, and it is called several times in a row: once when the floor is known and again on every resize while the page still reads itself under it, up to `HOLD_ASKS` times. They do not answer in the order they were sent. `refused` reaches the status bar as `en.shell.errors.windowFloor` (`src/App.tsx:3410`), so one refusal among them leaves an error standing about a floor the window took.

The fourth, idle memory under 400 MB with a video paused, was measured by nothing, so a regression in it could not be seen.

  - **Two ways to measure it wrongly, both taken first.** Summing RSS gave 646 MB, because three processes that share pages count each shared page three times; PSS is the only honest sum. And `appEnv` disarms the webview workarounds on purpose, so specs do not test a configuration no user gets: for a memory number that inverts, and the first version of the check read 520 MB, a third high, guarding a number nobody runs.

  - Section 7's remaining unmeasured claim is cold start under two seconds. `webview-paint-check.js` reports 698 to 2102 ms and its own header calls that a starting point rather than the measurement. Filed as P2.

Line 255 does `cursorTo(toplevel, 3)`, which sends the picture there, and the next line seeks into that follow while it is travelling.
  - Same defect as N89, in a file touched then for a different reason: `keyboard.spec.js` got the shared helper but not the follow wait, because its visible fault at the time was the unguarded seek. **Fixing the first is what let the second be seen.**
  - **The guard paid twice**, which is the argument for writing failure messages this way: first it turned a confusing downstream assertion into a message naming both numbers, then that message made the next defect readable in seconds instead of by deduction. The corollary is worth keeping too: fixing a defect in a file does not mean the file is sound, only that the next one is now visible.

Tracks, selected and visible are all correct; only the count is wrong.
  - The app's log gives the rest. After `video: the picture is drawn 640 by 360` there are **two** refreshes, one from `video::open` and one from the picture (N88), both reporting `0 chars`, and then nothing for thirty seconds. So N92's observer armed and never fired, which means `sub-text` never became non-empty in that window.
  - **What that says, and what it does not.** It says mpv had the track added and selected and reported no rendered line for thirty seconds on a paused player. It does **not** yet say whether the user would see a subtitle on that frame: N88's entry concluded the subtitle does reach the frame from the track being added and selected, and this is reason to hold that conclusion more loosely rather than to reverse it.
  - **Why it is not fixed here.** It does not reproduce on this machine, where the same paused open reports real counts every time, so a cure could be written but not verified, and the difference is not yet understood: whether mpv renders later than thirty seconds, never renders on that host, or renders while reporting nothing.
  - What would settle it: making the app ask mpv to redraw after `sub-add` and seeing whether the runner's count fills in, which is a product change and needs the reproduction first. The diagnostics for it are now all in place, which is what N82, N86, N88, N92 and N95 were for.

  - The keys are the reference's own defaults rather than taste: `R`, `E`, `D`, `T`. Sublore already follows those letters where it has them, lead-in and lead-out being `C` and `V`.

- [x] **N106 The other five timing shortcuts are on the numpad, and the parser has one now (2026-09-10, PR #236).** `play selection`, `play before`, `play after`, `previous line` and `next line` sit on the reference's numpad 5, 1, 3 and Ctrl with 8 and 2, and they carry those keys now. `parseAccelerator` gained one branch, `Num 5` and `Num5` alike, returning a `code` chord.
  - **Which property to compare on was already argued in that file**, and the argument carried: a letter matches on `key` because on AZERTY Ctrl+A must be the key labelled A, whose `code` is `KeyQ`; a digit matches on `code` because `key` carries the glyph the layout puts there. For the numpad the same reason holds twice over, and the second is stronger: it is **NumLock**, not the layout, that moves the glyph, and with it off `Numpad5`'s `key` is not a digit at all. So `code`.
  - **The measurement the criteria asked for answered, and it changed the test rather than the plan.** Pressing `KP_5` under Xvfb delivers **two** keydowns, `{"code":"NumLock","key":"NumLock"}` and then `{"code":"Numpad5","key":"5"}`: xdotool turns NumLock on around the press, so `key` is the digit. The consequence is about the proof, not the code: **with `KP_5` the `code`-to-`key` mutation stays green**, because with NumLock on the two comparisons agree. A test that cannot tell them apart repeats the choice instead of proving it.
  - So the test presses **`KP_Begin`**, what the same physical key sends with NumLock off, where `key` is not a digit at all: matched on `key` the spec reads 7 passing and **1 failing**, matched on `code` it reads 8 passing. It is also the harder of the two states, so the numpad is now covered with NumLock either way and not only in the one the harness imposes.
  - Verified on Linux: full gate green including clippy on `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, battery 75 spec files and **441** tests, green twice in a row, the guard checked against a counted run reading 441 exactly. No new test: the numpad presses live inside N105's own.

Alone it passes three times out of three and the next three batteries were green, so it is the N13 family and not this branch. What its kept log said is now N108.
- [x] **N107 The reference's default keys read whole, and every one SubLore can express is laid (2026-09-10, PRs #237 and #238).** Third pass of the same triage: rather than fish out one command at a time, the reference's entire default table was read against every accelerator SubLore declares. **53 of 119 commands carried one.**
  - **Two accords were claimed twice.** `Ctrl+1` by `video.to-cue-start` and `video.jump-cue-start`, `Ctrl+2` by the matching pair, and `commandFor` returns the **first** registry match, so the Video menu drew a key that ran the Timing menu's copy. No user could see it, because both bodies seek to the same place and the guards agree in practice; the risk was the two drifting and the key silently following the wrong one.
  - **The first plan for that fix was wrong, and the reading that corrects it is worth keeping.** It said to have both menus list one id. `command-registry.spec.js` has asserted the opposite from the start, that no id is drawn twice. And the private interface spec lists the Timing menu in full **without** these two: they are Video menu items there, as in the reference, whose audio strip has no command that moves the video to a line's boundary at all. So the Timing copies came from neither source that menu is built from, and they are gone.
  - **A new check walks every menu, collects every accelerator drawn and asks that each belongs to one command.** Written before the fix, against the binary that still held the defect, it went red naming both pairs. Its absence assertion carries a positive control, because one that reads nothing passes by blindness: more than twenty accelerators collected, and `Ctrl+S` against `file-save`.
  - **Five commands had no key at all**, stop and the four nudges, and they have the reference's own now: `Num 8`, `Num 4`, `Num 6`, `Num 7`, `Num 9`, taken from its `Always` set, which is the binding that answers whatever has the focus there. That rule is what N105 and N106 already followed without naming it, and it is named here.
  - **Laying them needed the arrows fixed first, measured and not assumed.** With NumLock off the numpad's 4 arrives as `code` "Numpad4" with a `key` of **"ArrowLeft"**, and 6 and 8 do the same for Right and Up. The parser matched arrows and named keys on `key`, so numpad 4 was already answering the `Left` accelerator and stepping a frame, and adding `Num 4` beside it would have left one press matching two commands with registry order deciding. Arrows and `Delete` match on `code` now.
  - **The check for it was wrong first, in this file's own dominant shape.** It pressed the numpad's 4 then a real Left and asked for one frame of movement, but it read at the first sign of movement, which the first of two steps satisfies, so it passed with the defect in place. Reading a **settled** playhead makes it red: 4.93 where one frame back is 4.967, which is two steps. Ninth of the read-before-ready family, and the first one written rather than inherited.
  - **A side effect caught by the specs after it:** the numpad's 4 is the start nudge now, so the check edits the document on its way past and left the fixture moved for the boundary walk that follows. It undoes the nudge, and the undo emptying the history is what says the edit was the only one.
  - **What is left is a shape and not a key**, filed as N109: the reference gives one command more than one accord and SubLore's `accelerator` is one string, so `Num Enter` beside commit's `G` and `Ctrl+Num 4` beside the frame step's `Left` cannot be expressed without it.
  - Verified on Linux: full gate green including clippy on `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, battery 75 spec files and **443** tests, green twice in a row, the guard raised against a counted run reading 443 exactly.

  - **What was missing.** §8.5's table gives the scope v1 to "Double-click selects the format tokenizer's notion of a word, not a whitespace-delimited run; past the end it selects the final token". The current line was a plain `textarea` with no handler, so a double-click inside `{\an8\pos(320,50)\fad(200,200)}` took one character of it.
  - **What it does now.** `src/tokens.ts` gives the box the format's boundaries: a braced run is one token whatever is inside it, `\N`, `\n` and `\h` are one token each, and everything else is a word, a run of spaces or a run of punctuation. The browser still does the hit test and the handler widens what it found.
  - **The assumptions, declared.** A braced run is one token even when it is prose rather than a tag, because the format gives braces their meaning and not their contents, which is the rule `drawnText` already holds; an unclosed brace takes the rest of the line, as a renderer does with it.
  - **The other §8.5 v1 rows were already built:** multi-line and wrapped, Tab and Shift+Tab moving focus, Shift+Enter inserting the break at `CurrentLine.tsx` with N113 cited, and the paste normalising its line endings in `plan_text_write`.
  - **Proved by taking it away, and it nearly proved nothing.** The first mutation left `tokenAt` imported and unused, `pnpm build` failed on TS6133, and the check ran against the binary from before the mutation and passed. Kept alive with `void tokenAt`, the build succeeded and the check went red by name: `Expected "{\an8\pos(320,50)\fad(200,200)}"`, `Received "\"`. One character against thirty. A mutation that does not reach the build proves nothing.
  - **A contradiction in the record, left to the owner.** §8.5 ends "Sublore v1 draws the last five rows" of the reference's editor context menu. §3.9, the section that owns context menus, says "**Two**, both drawn from the same registry" and names the grid row menu and the grid header menu, which is `later`. The app has the two §3.9 names and no menu on the text box. §3.9 owns the subject and is explicit, so the reading taken is that §8.5's closing line is stale. Nothing is blocked either way.
  - Verified on Linux: full gate green including clippy on `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, two batteries at 81 spec files with the count guard silent, every CI job green on the PR. The count moves to 491.

What was missing was `undo_label()` beside `can_undo()`, the pair crossing the IPC, and a phrase per kind. `EditNameDto` is a total match over `EditKind` with no wildcard, so a new kind breaks the build rather than reaching the menu unnamed.
  - **No English crosses the boundary**, which `error.rs` states as the rule: the wire carries the kind and, for the two kinds a reader tells apart by it, the parameter's own name. The style editor's twenty-two fields are deliberately not on the label, and neither is the cue index: the reference names the edit, not the row.

The check reads the row the cursor is on now, which is the question it was actually asking. Three batteries after that are green at 80 spec files with the count guard silent.
  - **A hole in the gate, closed with it.** `cargo fmt --check --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml` covers only the `sublore` package; the root manifest is the workspace and lists `crates/*` beside `src-tauri`, so the CI form, plain `cargo fmt --check` from the root, is the one that covers everything. It is what the gate runs from now on. N148 found it by being refused at the pre-commit hook.

- [ ] **N149 The undo depth is 200 where the owner's standing rule names 50, and two private documents disagree about which is right (filed 2026-09-11).** Found by walking all 48 owner answers against the tree, the sweep N148 came out of.
  - **The two records.** `owner-answers-2026-09-03.md` lists the reference numbers Sublore inherits and names "undo depth 50" among them. Interface-spec §8.3's "What Sublore takes" paragraph names three things and the depth is not one of them: the 30-second idle timeout, the same-description rule, and the switching-field rule. `history.rs:19-21` ships `MAX_ENTRIES = 200` and `MAX_BYTES = 8 MiB`, whichever is hit first.
  - **Not built as the reference on purpose is possible, but nobody wrote it down.** `find-replace-tasks.md:25` cites the 200 and the 8 MB as a fact about the implementation, not as a decision to diverge.
  - **Not taken unilaterally, and the reason is the user's.** Moving 200 down to 50 takes capability away from a translator with nothing gained; moving the record instead costs nothing. Under the standing rule a gap is work and not a question, but here the two documents already contradict each other, so there is no single reference behaviour to build: one of the records has to be corrected first, and which one is the owner's.
  - **What is not at issue:** exposing it as a preference. §9.6 names the set v1 exposes, lead-in, lead-out, new-cue duration, CPS limit and the interface language, and the undo depth is not in it. That part is decided.
  - **The rest of the sweep found nothing else open.** 46 was the one decided-and-unbuilt answer and is N148. 9 (automatic scene detection) and 25 (spectrogram) are answered yes and recorded in the same document as milestones of their own, and the spec scopes both `later`. 29 is built in full (N104), 47 is built, 40 and 44 hold. Of the named numbers, lead-in 100, lead-out 350, new-cue 3000, recent files 16 and the 8 px drag and snap are all in the tree, and the 300 ms adjacent gap belongs to §9.13's timing post-processor, which is `later` in full.

The 80 tests already in `session.rs` pass unchanged with the widened variants, which is the evidence that a list of one behaves as the single-cue write did.

- [x] **N147 Two refusals could stand at once, and Discard answered the wrong one (2026-09-11, PR #275).** Found by re-reading N116's own diff: adding a third refusal mark made the pair that was already there look wrong.
  - **What it did.** `useSubtitleFile.ts` remembers a refused open so Discard knows what to do about it, and it keeps one mark per route. They were not exclusive, and `discardAndOpen` reads them in a fixed order. Dirty document, New refused, then Open refused, then Discard: it took the New branch, made the empty document, and the file the user had just asked for never opened. The work went anyway.
  - **A second way in, found with it:** a successful open cleared only the path, so a New refused earlier left Discard alive over the file that had just arrived, and pressing it would have blanked it.
  - **What it does now.** Every refusal clears the two marks that are not its own, and `applyOpened` clears all three, so a document that arrives leaves nothing standing refused behind it.
  - **Proved by taking it away**, in the build: with `setBlockedNew(false)` removed from the open's refusal path, `new-document.spec.js`'s new check goes red by name, `timed out waiting for the file the user asked for to open once the edit was discarded`, because the empty document arrived instead.
  - Verified on Linux: full gate green including clippy on `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, and four batteries, three green at 78 spec files with the count guard silent. The fourth is in the entry below. The count moves to 480.

- [x] **N146 The wheel's part-frame crossed from one picture to the next, and the listener behind it was rebuilt on every render (2026-09-11, PR #273).** Found by re-reading this session's own diff, not by a failure: the battery was green through all of it.
  - **The churn.** `VideoControls.tsx` attached its native `wheel` listener in an effect whose dependencies were `[enabled, onStep]`, and `onStep` is written inline at the call site in `App.tsx`, so it is a new function on every render. The playhead renders several times a second while a picture plays, so the listener was taken off and put back that often. It now reads the caller's step through a ref, kept the way `App.tsx` keeps its command registry, and depends on `enabled` alone, which changes when a media opens or closes and at no other time.
  - **The defect underneath it.** `wheelRest` holds the part of a frame a gesture has not spent, which is what makes two half notches step the frame one whole notch would (N133). Nothing cleared it when the media changed, so half a notch on one picture completed the first half notch on the next and that first gesture stepped two frames.
  - **Proved by taking it away, in the build.** `video-frame-keys.spec.js` now scrolls half a notch, closes the picture and opens it again, then scrolls half a notch and three: three frames with the half dropped, four with it carried. With `wheelRest.current = 0` removed and `pnpm e2e:build` run, it reads `Expected: < 0.11666666666666667, Received: 0.13`, which is four frames where three were asked for. Put back, green.
  - Verified on Linux: full gate green including clippy on `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, and two batteries of 77 spec files with the count guard silent, on the very build that shipped. The count moves to 473.

- [x] **N145 A match ended at the next kept character, and ate the block in between (2026-09-11, PR #272).** Found by re-reading this session's own diff, not by a failure: nothing was red.
  - **What it did.** With the tags skipped, a match's offsets are mapped back into the real text. The end was mapped to where the **next kept character** begins, which with a block sitting right after the match is the far side of that block: `ab{\i1}cd` searched for `ab` and replaced deleted the `{\i1}` with it. Formatting nobody touched, gone.
  - **What it does now.** The end is one past the match's **own last character**. A block *inside* a match still goes with it, which is the reference's behaviour and what the split line's check already read; a block after it stays.
  - The fixture gained a fifth cue for the case and the walks that count matches moved with it. **The mutation is the old mapping put back**, and the check then reads `The beacon went out.` where it wants `The beacon{\i0} went out.`
  - Verified on Linux: full gate green including clippy on `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, battery 77 spec files and 472 tests, green twice in a row.

A toggle that silently did nothing is a failure now rather than a quiet return to the race.

- [x] **N143 `cue-insert`'s seek is undone on the runner, and the fix is to take the race away (2026-09-11, PR #270).** Twice today the CI battery came back with one red spec and the same message: `the seek to 6s never landed: the playhead settled at 2.13s`, which is row one's own start plus a frame. The shape is N89's, the follow that moves the picture to the cursor's line arriving after a seek sent while it was still on its way.
  - **What is already right about that test.** It moves the cursor, waits, then seeks, and its own comment says why. The wait is `settledPlayhead`, which proves the picture has stopped and nothing more; `playhead.spec.js` and `timing-continuous.spec.js` both wait at the follow's destination instead, and say in their comments that a follow which has not started yet is a picture that has stopped.
  - **So the obvious fix is to copy their wait, and it does not hold up.** Three attempts, all on this machine, all inconclusive or worse: the follow delayed by 1.5 s in the build, the same at 6 s, and the same again with the cursor moved to another row first so the follow certainly fires. The old wait stayed **green** through all three, so the defect was never reproduced and no mutation ever proved the stricter wait does anything.
  - **And the stricter wait failed here for a reason worth writing down:** with two moves, `the follow to row 2's start never landed: the playhead settled at 0s`. In this spec, on this machine, moving the cursor does not move the picture at all. Either the follow is not firing here or it is firing where nothing reads it, and until that is understood a stricter wait in this file is a new flake rather than a fix.
  - **Then the runner answered it.** Its own log for that spec reads `playhead ... at 6.000 s` twice, then `at 2.133 s`, then the insert: the seek landed and the follow arrived after it and took the picture back to row one's start. So the diagnosis was right and only the fix was unprovable.
  - **Closed by removing the race rather than by racing better (PR #270).** The check turns the follow off through the Video menu before it seeks, and reads the menu back to say it is off. A setting that is off cannot arrive late on any machine, and this test was never about the follow: it is about a line timed from the playhead. The mutation is the toggle made to store the value it already had, which reddens the new precondition and `video-transport.spec.js`'s own check of that toggle.

- [x] **N142 Six guards taken away one at a time, and what each one caught (2026-09-11).** The sweep N140 and N141 started, carried through the rules CONTRIBUTING.md calls hard. No code changed: every mutation was put in the build, run, and taken out again.
  - **The atomic write (section 3.2).** `replace` made to truncate the destination and write into it: `a_crash_never_tears_a_file_it_overwrites` red, and `concurrent_saves_leave_one_complete_version` and `a_read_only_directory_reports_permission_denied` with it. Three independent tests on one rule.
  - **The database's durability (section 3.4).** `synchronous` dropped from FULL to NORMAL, which is exactly "may lose a committed transaction on power loss": `the_connection_pragmas_are_the_ones_that_were_asked_for` red by name.

- [x] **N141 The backup rule had one guard, and the battery was not it (2026-09-11, PR #269).** Found the way N140 was, by taking the guard away: with `save_with_backup` made to archive nothing, `e2e:close-gate` went red by name, `e2e:quit-gate` stayed green, and **the whole battery stayed green**.

It reads the store after that save and asks for **one** backup for that file, holding the bytes that were there and not the ones just written, named for the file and the second. The bytes are read off the file before the overwrite rather than assumed to be the source's.
  - **Two mutations:** no backup at all, and a backup rewritten with the new bytes, which is the weaker defect N74 paid for. Both red. `e2e:close-gate` stays green beside it: this is a second guard, not a moved one.

Both said 111.5 px at the size the app opens at, and **the half pixel is what made the rounding a coin toss**: the sash declares `Math.round` of its maximum and the check rounds the measured height, and the two landed either side once in four.

  - **Built the way the line's own combos are**, down to the keys: the down arrow opens the list, the arrows walk it, Enter picks, Escape closes the list before anything else. A term is remembered when it is **searched with** and not while it is typed, most recent first, once each, sixteen kept, and the two fields' lists do not mix.

A search term is a fragment of the user's own writing, which is the rule the edit log already follows when it counts a line's characters rather than naming them. If the owner wants it persisted it is a line in the store, and `find-recent-terms-tasks.md` says which.

It is the rail's own empty space now, read off the page rather than off a screenshot: the rail runs y 69..405 and `NAV.rail` answers anywhere in it no node covers, so y 300 keeps a hundred pixels of margin either way.

The surface's y came back 124 against the 202 it wanted.
  - **So the strip takes air**: 0.45rem of button padding and a 0.3rem gap, which is 913 px of 1024 under this machine's type and 963 under the runner's, one row in both. The wrap stays as the net and is no longer a coin toss.

What is missing is the command: with the grid focused there is no way to zoom the wave at all, while the pan pair beside it has `A` and `F` as registry accelerators and says in its own comment that it is keyboard-only "like the reference keeps them".

A command with no route is worse than none.
  - **What would close it:** the owner naming a key for fit, or a decision that the strip may carry an eighteenth button. Either way the other two go in with it, as one piece.
- [ ] **N136 Three edges drag where the reference drags one, and that is the owner's to keep or drop (filed 2026-09-11).** Found by the §1 pass of the panel triage. The interface spec's §1.2 opens with "**Exactly one edge in the whole window drags: the waveform panel's bottom edge**", cites the reference for it, and adds that every other split there is a fixed proportion the user cannot touch, naming video versus tools width and waveform versus current line.
  - **Sublore drags three:** the video panel's width (`edge="video"`), the waveform's height (`edge="waveform"`) and the grid's (`edge="grid"`), all in `App.tsx` and all with stored positions, floors and ceilings.
  - **It is not a slip.** The spec's own line says it matches what Sublore ships, written when Sublore had one; T2 and T5 added the others deliberately, D1 says the video edge is the one the owner asked for, and `dividers.spec.js` and `interface-scale.spec.js` are built on all three.
  - **So it is not work until the owner says so:** taking two edges away is a change to the shell's shape, which is M2.0 and T9, and those are his. What this entry does is stop the divergence being silent. Either the edges stay and §1.2's sentence is a record of the reference rather than a rule for Sublore, or they go and three specs go with them.
- [x] **N116 The subtitles a video carries inside it, opened from File with no picker (2026-09-10, closed 2026-09-11, PR #274).** Found by the menu triage's File pass: the reference draws it between "Open with encoding" and "Open from backup", and a grep over every private document found it recorded nowhere, in no scope. So it was a gap, and a gap is work.
  - **The ground was laid before today, deliberately.** `AudioTrack.ff_index` carries the comment "ffmpeg's index for the stream, which is what an extraction maps with `-map 0:n`", and the waveform's extraction does exactly that. `embedded_subtitle_tracks` reads the same `track-list` for streams of type `sub`, and `subtitle::from_video::extract` hands ffmpeg the one that was asked for.
  - **The assumptions, declared rather than asked about.** A container with several text streams is answered by taking the first, which is what the reference does. Only text codecs count, by allow list, so a video carrying nothing but picture tracks leaves the command greyed. A document read out of a video has no file and is unsaved from the moment it appears, the same choice the transcription adoption makes: the window reads `* Untitled - Sublore` and the mark is what stops those cues being closed without a word.
  - **Three mutations, each in the build, each red by name.** The validator made always true reddens both greying checks; `.rev()` in front of the stream choice reddens "takes the first of two streams"; `ass` mapped to SRT reddens the ASS check with `Expected substring "{\i1}styled one{\i0}", Received "<font size=\"24\"><i>styled one</i></font>"`, which is the loss that check exists to catch.
  - **The allow list is a unit test and not a battery check, and the reason is measured.** ffmpeg refuses to encode a text subtitle into a picture one, so no fixture can carry a picture-only track: `Subtitle encoding currently only possible from text to text or bitmap to bitmap`.
  - **Two guards caught the new command before the diff was read back**, which is what they are for: `command-registry.spec.js` keeps every entry with its greyed state and `menu-separators.spec.js` keeps File's groups in order. Both updated.
  - **Three defects found by re-reading my own diff, not by a failure.** The extraction read stdout to the end and only then stderr, so an ffmpeg that filled the stderr pipe would have stopped writing stdout and the read would never have ended; stderr is drained on a thread of its own now. The session lock was held across the ffmpeg run, so a container that hung it would have held the document's mutex with it; the unsaved gate is asked before the extraction and again under the lock that replaces the document. And the size cap was read after the exit status, which would have called a stream that is too big a failed extraction.
  - Verified on Linux: full gate green including clippy on `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, two batteries of 78 spec files with the count guard silent. The count moves to 479.
  - **Left open beside it, for its own PR:** the three refusal flags in `useSubtitleFile.ts` are not exclusive. The new one was made exclusive in both directions; the two older ones overlap already, and `discardAndOpen` reads them in order. Dirty document, New refused, then Open refused, then Discard: it takes the New branch and makes an empty document, and the file the user asked for never opens.

- [x] **N117 The second window is not v1, and the answer was already given (filed 2026-09-10, closed by triage 2026-09-11).** `app/new_window` opens another application window with its own project context, drawn in the reference's File menu above Quit. Filed as unrecorded; it is recorded, one line above where the filer looked.
  - **`owner-answers-2026-09-03.md` question 3: "One window | Permanent for v1. No detachable video window."** The first sentence is the general rule and it covers this: a second application window is not one window.
  - **The nuance, written down rather than smoothed over.** §12's question 3 asks specifically about "the detached video window and everything around it", and the answer's second sentence answers that. The filer grepped for `new_window` and for a second window and found nothing, which is true; what was there to find was a rule stated more broadly than the question that prompted it. **So the lesson is about how to grep the answers**, and it is the same lesson N148 and N150 taught from the other direction: an answer can be narrower than its question or wider, and only reading it settles which.
  - Nothing is built and nothing changes. If the owner ever wants a second window it reopens on his word, and the note at filing still stands: the shell's state, the layout store, the player and the data home are all built around one window today.

- [x] **N114 The menu triage, the dimension after the keys, complete (2026-09-10).** With the keyboard triage closed, what is left to compare is what the menus draw and in what order, which the 2026-09-05 ruling makes a defect when it differs and not a question.
  - **The method, and the trap in it:** the reference's menus are read by merging `default_menu_platform.json` over `default_menu.json`, because File, Edit and Subtitle in the base file are placeholders. Without that merge `subtitle/save` reads as being in no menu at all, which is how the N109 reading nearly went wrong.
  - **The authority is the reference.** The private interface spec is the record of a reading, not the source: where it adds something the reference does not have, that is a Sublore decision and not a gap.
  - **Two menus checked and clean, recorded so the next pass does not reopen them.** The bar's eight titles match in order. View draws Sublore's own waveform toggles and interface scales beyond the spec's table, which the code's own comment already declares as a placement rather than a drift. Audio draws the tracks flat where the spec's table wants them in a submenu, and **the reference has no such submenu at all**, so the spec is the one adding it.
  - **All eight done.** Subtitle and Edit gave an order defect each (N115, N118), Help a layout one (N119), File two unrecorded features (N116, N117), and Timing, Video, Audio and the bar itself were clean with their divergences already declared in the code.
- [x] **N113 Return commits and goes on, which is what the reference binds it to (2026-09-10, PR #243).** Return in the text box committed the line and stopped. In the reference it runs `grid/line/next/create`, three things in order: commit what is pending, go to the next line, and make one when there is nowhere to go.
  - **Sublore already had all three**, in `nextLine`: it flushes the editors, moves the cursor, and past the last line inserts a cue starting where that one ends, for the preferences' new-cue duration. So this was a binding and not a behaviour, which is why it is small.
  - **Shift+Return is unchanged** and still writes a real line break where the format holds one, which stays the only way to break a line in SRT. Nobody loses a capability they had.
  - **Two checks moved with it, and that is worth naming rather than burying.** `current-line.spec.js` used Return as "commit the box" throughout, so the commit check now asserts both commands and reads the line Return made, including its start being where the line before it ends; and the undo check takes two steps, the new line first and the text second, because two edits is what Return now makes. Six checks were red at first and fixing those two fixed all six, which says the other four were reading a document the first had changed rather than testing Return.
  - Mutation in the build: with Return committing and staying, the commit check reddens on the missing `subtitle_insert`.
  - Verified on Linux: full gate green including clippy on `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`, battery 75 spec files and **447** tests, green twice in a row. Criteria in `sublore-meta/docs/enter-goes-on-tasks.md`, which also records the one place the reference has no answer to copy, a real line break in SRT, and the assumption taken there.
- [x] **N112 The four colours are commands now, not buttons alone (2026-09-10, PR #242).** They existed in `CurrentLine.tsx` as four buttons that open a picker and write `\c`, `\2c`, `\3c` and `\4c`, and they are in v1 by the 2026-09-03 ruling. What they were not is **registry commands**: no menu item, no key, and their greying computed inside the component rather than by the registry. The reference has them as commands on `Alt-1` to `Alt-4`.

Sublore's is a popover anchored to a button, and the command opens it over that colour's own button, which is on screen whenever the command is enabled because one condition gates both. No second placement rule.
  - **One thing that made necessary, found by the check rather than by thinking:** the picker's Escape handler is on the picker, and a command run from the keyboard leaves the caret in the text box, which is outside it. So the picker takes the focus when it opens, and Escape now closes it however it was opened. The file's own comment had already said the div was made focusable for exactly this reason.
  - **The check asks which picker opened**, reading the button that reports itself expanded, and asserts the command is enabled first so a greyed command and a key that never arrived cannot read alike. Swapping the outline and shadow keys in the build leaves a picker opening and reddens only that assertion.

The measurement that settled the second was a probe of what `alt+3` actually delivers: `code` "Digit3" with `altKey`, exactly as declared, which ruled the key out and left the focus.

- [x] **N111 The drawn size was read before it had stopped changing, and the tree a red battery kept says so (2026-09-10, PR #240).** `video-aspect.spec.js` read 640 for the anamorphic fixture where the answer is 1280. One open wrote **two** lines at 19:06:14, `video: the picture is drawn 640 by 360` and then `1280 by 360`: mpv reports the frame the file stores before it knows the pixel aspect, `tell_picture` sends every change, and `drawnSize` returned as soon as any size had arrived. Tenth of the read-before-ready family.
  - **Why it reads as a flake and is not one:** a first report equal to what the interface was already told is dropped rather than sent, so the intermediate appears only when the file before it left a different size behind.
  - **A wrong turn of mine, corrected by measuring rather than argued away.** An instrumented run showed `[null, 1280]`, no intermediate, and for a moment that looked like the diagnosis failing. It is not: that run simply did not produce one, and the kept log proves it exists. **A reproduction that does not happen is not a refutation** when the defect is a race.
  - The wait is on the count of payloads standing still, which is a different fact from the size the check asserts, and its limit is written down rather than implied: 400 ms, more than an order of magnitude wider than the measured gap, and a slower machine would still be read early.

Nothing was lost from git, and the entry is put back exactly as it stood; it is recorded here rather than quietly repaired, because a backlog that can lose an open defect silently is worse than one with a scar in it. What would stop it recurring is anchoring an edit on both ends of the entry it means to touch, which is what the replacements after this one do.
- [ ] **N108 The Play gesture left no trace at all, and what that means had to be deduced instead of read (filed 2026-09-10).** Found in the log N86 kept from N106's one red battery of `silent-machine.spec.js`. The click on the transport produced **no** `playback: asked mpv to play` line, and nothing else after the waveform finished peaking.
  - **Corrected while filing, because the first reading was wrong.** The entry first said the line was missing because `set_pause` logs after `self.handle()?` and `self.loaded_duration()?`, so a gesture with no file loaded returns early and says nothing. That is true of the code and **false of this failure**: reading `open` shows a successful open sets `status = Ready` and `duration = Some`, and nothing resets them at end of file, so `loaded_duration()` would have answered and the line would have been written. The absence therefore says something sharper: **the command never reached the player at all**, so the suspect is the click or the page, not mpv.
  - **The work is still worth doing, for a different reason than the one first written.** Reaching that conclusion took reading three functions to rule out the early returns. With the line moved above them, the absence would have been unambiguous on sight, which is the whole point of the N83, N95, N96 and N102 chain: a log that answers without a reading.
  - It is also the third case N96 cannot name. That entry separated a command mpv refused from one that never arrived; a command that never reached the player is a third state, and today it looks exactly like the second.
  - **Not yet provable through a gesture**, which is honest to say now rather than at the end: with no file open the transport is not drawn and `video.play` is greyed, and the harness has no IPC back door on purpose, every route ending at `runCommand`. So the criteria have to name a reachable state before the code moves.
  - **Recurred 2026-09-11 in a local battery, and the log gives the instrument's own blind spot.** `silent-machine.spec.js` failed once on "keeps playing past the second where the audio device would have ended it" while N147's batteries ran; the spec is green three out of three alone on the same build and the three batteries after it are green, so it is this family and not that change. The kept log reads the same as the one this entry was filed from: the video chosen, the picture drawn 640 by 360, the waveform peaked, and then nothing at all.
  - **And a reading that was made again and was wrong again, recorded rather than quietly dropped.** On seeing that log I read `set_pause` and concluded the silence had a third cause: the line sits below `self.handle()?` and `self.loaded_duration()?`, so an early return there would say nothing. That is the very reading the second bullet of this entry corrected when it was filed, and it is false here for the same reason: the log shows the video opened and the picture drawn, so `duration` is `Some` and `loaded_duration()` would have answered. **The recurrence adds no new cause.** What it adds is a second sighting with the same signature and a reminder that this entry's own correction has to be read before its log is.

  - **The mutation was not invented.** N1c's acceptance criterion says the check must fail "when the plugin path is restored", so the cfg on `chooser.rs`'s Linux `pick` was flipped to send Linux back through `tauri-plugin-dialog`, which is rfd, which is the thread.
  - **Caught, with exactly the right discrimination.** The acceptance assertion went red and named the culprit by symbol: `rfd::backend::gtk3::utils::GtkGlobalThread::new`, which is what the file's own header predicts. **The positive control stayed green** ("eu-stack sees the main thread iterating GTK, so it can see a thread that does"), so the mutation broke the product and not the measurement. That distinction was written into the criteria before the run, because a detector that stops seeing is indistinguishable from a thread that stopped existing.
  - Worth noting for the next sieve: this file was already built better than the average. A check that asserts an absence needs a positive control or it can pass by being blind, and this one has had it all along.
  - Restored with `touch`, tree clean, 20 of 20 green. Verified on Linux.

- [x] **N92 `sub-text` was sampled where it has to be observed (2026-09-10, PR #227).** N88 works and is not enough, which the log of run 34474594688 shows in three lines: after `the picture is drawn`, the first refresh reports `no line at the playhead` and the second, the one N88 added, reports `0 chars at the playhead`. On this machine mpv always renders in time, so the flag is never armed and the observer never fires: it could not be measured directly. With the condition replaced by `true`, `preview.spec.js` goes from 10 `mpv holds the document` lines to **17**. So the observer does fire and does cause a refresh, and **the bound is what keeps it from firing seven more times in one spec**, which is exactly the concern written down before the code was.

Unlike N51 and N52 it has a patched release, so there was something to take and it was taken.

The gate now compares the backup's bytes with the overwritten file's, requires exactly one backup for one save, and asserts the name carries the second it was taken. Against the same mutation it goes red; on clean code it is 13 of 13.

With `text.rs` reading a pure-CRLF file as LF, so a save normalises the line endings, five crate tests go red and the spec's two CRLF fixtures fail at the status line, before the byte comparison is even reached. The fifth, that no feature writes across the user's folders, was written and never observed.

The mutation did the real damage too: five `.peaks` files landed in `fixtures/video/`, and were removed and the folder compared against the listing taken before it.

- [x] **N71 The clean shutdown check, sieved, came out clean too (2026-09-10).** Fourth and last of the close-path mutation passes, over `shutdown-check.js`'s five assertions. **No weak assertion.**
  - A gate that asks on a clean document reddens it at 1 of 5, and not on the dialog assertion: on the exit wait, because the dialog is modal and holds the process. That is what the file's own comment at lines 84 to 87 says the primary witness is, so the mutation confirms the design rather than finding anything.
  - **Two mutations could not reach it, and the reason is structural.** The exit code of a clean close is not the app's to choose: `sublore_lib::run()` never returns, because `app.run` exits the process itself, so a `std::process::exit(3)` placed after it still leaves 0, measured. And `app_handle.exit(3)` from the close arm is raised back as `ExitRequested` with a code, which the gate holds in order to ask, so that leaves 0 too, also measured.
  - **So that assertion's quarry is a crash, not a code**, and it catches one: with a fault on the way out the check reddens at 2 of 5 saying "exit code was null", which is N11's own signature. The fifth assertion, on surviving processes, has already been seen failing in the field, four times over 34 runs in N16.

That check guards the interval the gate's own dialog does not cover: between an answer and the close it asks for, the window is on screen and editable, and an edit committed there was never asked about. It is the subtlest data loss this project knows.
  - **No weak assertion.** The `AskAgain` arm closing instead of asking again reddens it at 3 of 8, with a message that names the defect. A gate save that writes nothing reddens it at 5 of 8, and not where I predicted: the app refuses to close while the document is dirty, so the check never reaches the file. That is the app being right, and it is written down rather than tidied.
  - **One mutation of mine was chosen badly, which is worth more than the pass.** Corrupting the **first** byte of the saved file left the check green at 8 of 8, and my first reading was that the byte assertion was weak. It is not: the first block is the one the late edit changes, so the corruption hid inside the only block that was already expected to differ. Corrupting the **last** byte reddens it at 7 of 8 with `differing [0,2]`. The assertion asks for exactly one changed block containing the edit, which is the right shape.
  - **The blind spot that remains, named:** a corruption inside the edited block that leaves the edit itself intact is invisible. Narrow, but "and nothing else moved" covers it, so it is written down. Numbers in `sublore-meta/docs/late-edit-proof-tasks.md`.

That command writes it **before** it calls anything, so a quit changed to close the window instead wrote it too. Measured: with the route swapped and the line left alone the check stayed **green at 17 of 17**, because the window's close has its own gate and the dialog appeared anyway. The check could not tell the two routes apart, which is the one thing its name says it knows. Same shape as N55.
