- [x] **N68 The line that keeps N11 closed is covered by no automated check (filed 2026-09-10).** A mutation pass over `close-gate-check.js`'s twelve assertions found three of them proved by their own mutation and a fourth that nothing catches. Neutering `shutdown_video` in `src-tauri/src/lib.rs` leaves the close gate green at 12 of 12, and leaves `e2e:scale` and `e2e:memory` green too, both of which open a video and close the window.
  - **Why they miss it, read rather than guessed.** The four shutdown checks never open a video at all: `close-gate-check.js`, `shutdown-check.js`, `quit-gate-check.js` and `close-gate-late-edit-check.js` do not mention one. The two that do open a video launch one app at a time on an idle machine, and `impl Drop for Player` shuts mpv down anyway when the process exits. What the explicit call buys is the **order** the gate's own comment names: mpv draws into a child of the window and has to be gone before the window is.
  - **That the order matters is measured.** `pnpm e2e:n11-battery 25 5`, the instrument this repository built for N11: the clean tree closed **0 of 25** runs on a signal, and with `shutdown_video` neutered **2 of 25**, with one more still running at teardown. So removing one line brings back the SIGSEGV on exit and every automated check stays green.
  - **What it needs.** Either a check that opens a video and closes the window under load, or the N11 battery in CI. The battery runs 25 launches in half a minute here, which is cheap, but its defect lives in contention and a runner's contention has never been measured. Same rule as the two budget checks: the runner's own number first, the guard after. Numbers in `sublore-meta/docs/close-gate-proof-tasks.md`.
  - **Closed by PR #207, verified on Linux, and it took the first option.** `pnpm e2e:video-shutdown` asserts the property rather than waiting for the crash: the close path stopped mpv and took the surface, once per close, with a video open and without one. It is deterministic, so it **is** a CI step, which is the difference between it and the two budget checks. Proved by mutation: with `shutdown_video` neutered the check goes red saying the line was there 0 times before the close and 0 after.
  - **Its own assertions caught two things I had wrong.** The line said itself three times for one close, because `Player::shutdown` latches `core_destroyed` and every later call answers true; it now comes from `take_surface`, which can tell the call that did the work from the ones after. And the criterion "with no video it says nothing" was backwards: the surface arrives with the app, not with a video, because `setup` creates it during Tauri's start. The criteria carry that correction rather than quietly reading the other way.
  - The N11 battery stays a hand run. Nothing needs it in CI now that the removal it was catching is caught deterministically.

- [x] **D1 The log kept a file more than three documents promised (filed and closed 2026-09-10).** `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`'s own comment said "Two archived files beside the active one" and the call said `KeepSome(3)`. `tauri-plugin-log` 2.9.0 counts the archived files and not the total: `remove_old_files` excludes the file it is writing to before it counts. So the folder held four files and eight megabytes where the comment, `README.md:142` and `docs/manual.md:126` all promised three and six.

  - Found while checking that the numbers the manual states are true, after adding text to that manual. - [x] **P2 Cold start is the last section 7 budget with no measurement (filed 2026-09-10).** `webview-paint-check.js:20` says its timings are "a starting point for CONTRIBUTING.md section 7's cold start, not" the measurement, and the range it reports, 698 to 2102 ms, straddles the two second budget. So the number that decides whether the budget is met has never been taken.
  - **What makes it harder than the memory one.** Cold start to interactive needs a definition of interactive that a script can read: the window mapped is too early and the waveform arriving is too late. The nearest honest mark is the first paint of the shell's own chrome, which `package-smoke.mjs` already waits for when it proves a package starts.
  - It also inherits the same two lessons P1 paid for: measure the configuration a user gets, with the workarounds armed, and say what the machine is, because under llvmpipe a first paint is not the first paint the budget describes.

React runs effects after the commit, so the mark lands with the chrome drawn or just after it, never before: conservative, and declared in the check's own header.
  - **The poll interval was the resolution of the answer.** At `waitFor`'s default 250 ms the same start read 505, 1009 and 506, which is the interval showing through the number. At 20 ms the numbers are the app's. Worth remembering the next time a budget is measured by polling.
  - **Neither budget check is a CI step, and the reason is now in `ci.yml` in one place.** `continue-on-error: true` on those steps does not make it safe to add them: `e2e-verdict.sh` reads the exit codes and turns any non-zero into a red job. That was checked rather than assumed, after assuming the opposite first. They go in when a runner's own figures exist.
  - **Section 7 is now measured end to end**, which is what its own title claims: the waveform's two, the 2000 line open, the idle memory (P1) and this.
- [x] **R5 The AppImage is attached to a release and nobody has ever run it (filed 2026-09-10, and the claim was false).** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` builds three Linux packages and smoke tests two: `package-smoke` installs and launches the deb in a clean Ubuntu image (line 554), `package-smoke-rpm` does the same for the rpm in a Fedora container (line 597). The AppImage has neither. `release` needs both smokes and attaches all three, so the one format that carries its own dependencies is the one no job has ever started.
  - The owner's hand run does not cover it either: `owner-run-v1.md` starts `target/debug/sublore`, which is the tree's own build and not a package.
  - **Why it is worth a job rather than a note.** An AppImage bundles what it needs, so the failure it hides is the opposite of the deb's: not a missing declaration but a library baked in against the wrong glibc, and it shows up on the user's machine and nowhere else. It is also the format a person who is not on Debian or Fedora will reach for.
  - **What it needs.** A job beside the other two: download `linux-appimage`, run it with `--appimage-extract` because a runner has no FUSE, and start the extracted binary far enough to prove it links, the way the other two prove their launch. Not written here because a CI job I cannot run is a job nobody has verified, which is the thing this entry is about.
  - **Struck the same day, because it is not true and I filed it without reading the script.** The AppImage **is** started. `package-smoke` downloads `linux-appimage` beside the deb (`ci.yml:582-586`) and hands both to `.github/scripts/package-smoke.sh`, which starts what the deb installed and then starts the AppImage with `APPIMAGE_EXTRACT_AND_RUN=1` under Xvfb (lines 74-78), through the same `package-smoke.mjs` that asserts the app puts its own window on screen. The script's own first line says so. The comment beside it even explains the order: after the deb, because linuxdeploy leaves the base X and GL stack to the host.
  - **What I got wrong and how.** I read the job names, saw two smokes for three packages, and wrote the conclusion down without opening the script either of them runs. The rule is to verify a claim before acting on it, and filing a backlog entry is acting on it. Left struck rather than deleted so the mistake is legible: the next reader who counts jobs will reach the same wrong answer.

- [x] **R4 A missing package would have failed on a path instead of on the package (filed and closed 2026-09-10).** `draft-release.sh` counted the three package kinds together, so one file of any kind satisfied it, and then attached them with three globs. `sh` leaves an unmatched glob alone, so with a kind missing `gh` received paths that do not exist. Measured with a directory holding only a `.deb`: `.../*.rpm` and `.../*.AppImage` went through verbatim.
  - **Closed by PR #202, verified on Linux.** Each kind is required by name and the refusal says which are missing; the attachment list is built from the files `find` returned, as positional arguments so a path with a space stays one path; the dry run lists what it would attach. Four cases run: empty names all three, deb only names rpm and AppImage, deb and rpm names AppImage, all three passes and lists them. The two older refusals still behave.
  - Found while proving R3 on the same path, which is the argument for exercising a release script rather than reading it: both failures are invisible until a tag exists, and both would have cost the owner the same hour.
- [x] **R3 A tag would have failed after the whole matrix, on a heading (filed and closed 2026-09-10).** `draft-release.sh` takes its notes from the changelog section named for the tag and refuses an absent or empty one, and the `release` job waits on `[vocabulary, check, e2e, package-smoke, package-smoke-rpm]`. So a tag pushed today would build for over an hour and fail on a documentation line. Proved with the script's own dry run: `draft-release: CHANGELOG.md has no "## v1.0.0" section to take notes from`.
  - **Closed by PR #201, verified on Linux.** `check-version.sh`, which runs in the five minute `vocabulary` job and already checks the version across three files, now also reads the section the release would read, and its message says to rename the Unreleased heading before tagging. All four outcomes were run against a fake tree through `SUBLORE_ROOT`, so no tag was created: missing section fails, empty section fails, real notes pass, off a tag passes. The empty case is checked on its own because a heading-only check would let the same late failure through.
  - **This does not do the owner's part**, and must not: renaming `## Unreleased` to the version is the version number, which is the owner's. What changed is when they find out, not who decides. Criteria in `sublore-meta/docs/release-changelog-gate-tasks.md`.
- [x] **N67 The rest of the late-answer audit, read and left alone (2026-09-10).** The audit that produced N60 to N66 covered every `invoke` in `src/`. What is left is unguarded and was judged not worth changing, each read rather than assumed: `useEventLog` (a later tick overwrites within a second), `useSubtitleFile`'s `subtitle_copy_cues` (only the shared error is written after the await), `usePreview`'s two toggles (a stale `failed` until the next toggle), `CurrentLine`'s eyedropper (a stale pick in the picker's preview fields, and picking twice at once means two portal dialogs), and `useTranscription`'s model download and its two cancels (nothing that reflects what a run is doing is written from them).
  - `useSubtitleFile`'s main path is **already guarded by a different mechanism**: it runs its commands through a serial queue rather than a counter, which is stronger, and its own comment at line 217 says why a queue was chosen over a busy flag.
  - Recorded so the next reader does not re-audit the same files. If one of these turns into a sighting, it gets its own entry with the sighting in it.
- [x] **N65 A double click on Start tears down the run it did not start (filed and closed 2026-09-10).** `src/hooks/useTranscription.ts`'s `start` had nothing stopping a second call while one was in flight. The Start button is greyed while a run is going (`TranscribePanel.tsx:159-166`), but a click is answered before React repaints, so a double click sent two starts. The backend refuses the second with `AsrErrorCode::Busy` (`src-tauri/src/asr/mod.rs:161,197`, with its own unit tests at 703 and 756), and the frontend's `catch` then cleared `running` and put an error banner up: the progress bar and Cancel vanish and an error appears over a transcription that is still going.
  - **Closed by PR #197, verified on Linux.** A `runningRef` says whether a run has been asked for and has not ended, read at the top of `start` and cleared where the run ends, on the done event, the error event and a failed start. One run at a time is decided in the hook rather than by a greying that is a repaint behind the click that produced it. `asr.spec.js` green, 34 passing lines; full battery green at 75 spec files and 439 tests.
  - **A demonstration is possible here and was not built, which is worth saying.** Unlike the races in N60 to N64, this one needs only two clicks close together, and `doubleClickAt` in `e2e/lib/input.js` already sends two 40 ms apart. Whether 40 ms beats a repaint under Xvfb was not measured. A spec named `asr-*.spec.js` gets the stub model from `wdio.conf.js`, so the machinery exists; what it costs is the panel and video setup `asr.spec.js` already does.
- [x] **N64 Two audio track switches land in whichever order they answer (filed and closed 2026-09-10).** `src/hooks/useAudioTracks.ts` wrote the track list from three places, the read that follows a new file, a switch's answer, and the re-read after a refused switch, and none of them said which request it belonged to. Two tracks picked in quick succession applied in answer order, so the mark could go back to the track before while the backend played the one after. The code's own comment says that is the state to avoid: "a menu that silently keeps its old mark would be claiming a track the app is not drawing."
  - **Closed by PR #196, verified on Linux.** An `asking` counter across all three writes, which also replaces the effect's `alive` flag: that flag ordered the effect against itself and not against a switch in flight. Full gate clean, battery green at 75 spec files and 439 tests. As with N60 to N63, no check demonstrates the race, because staging it needs a second pick inside the first one's flight.
  - Found by the audit of this shape that N60 started, and verified by reading the file before changing it. The audit's remaining findings are still to be read one at a time.
- [x] **N63 Nothing greys while a project command is running (filed 2026-09-10).** `busy` is computed in `src/hooks/useProject.ts` and **reaches nobody**: `ProjectRail.tsx` takes the whole project object and has no `disabled` anywhere in it, and no other file reads `busy`. So every item on the rail's context menu stays live while a command is in flight, and a second one can be asked for on top of the first.
  - The owner's 2026-09-03 ruling is that a command that cannot be used is greyed and that a greyed command must not run. Whether a project command that is already running makes the next one unusable is the question, and it is answered by what the backend does with a second one arriving mid-flight, which has not been read yet.
  - N62's counter (PR to follow) makes the overlap harmless rather than absent: the older answer applies nothing. This entry is the other half, which is whether the user should have been able to ask at all. Filed separately because one is correctness and the other is the interface.
  - **Closed 2026-09-10, PR #195, and the answer is that nothing should grey.** The backend keeps the project behind a `Mutex` (`src-tauri/src/project/mod.rs:34`) and every command takes it, so a second one arriving mid-flight waits and then does exactly what it says. The owner's ruling greys a command that **cannot** be used, and these can. With N62's counter the rail already shows the answer to the last thing asked.
  - So `busy` was not a wiring job left undone, it was state with no purpose, and it is removed: the type, the state, the five writes, the returned field, and the two `finally` blocks that held nothing else. Battery green at 75 spec files and 439 tests.
- [x] **N62 The project hook writes whatever answers last, and its busy flag reaches nobody (filed 2026-09-10).** `src/hooks/useProject.ts:113-134` runs every project command through one `run` helper with no generation counter, so two started close together apply in the order the backend replies: an open answering after a close puts back on screen a project the user just closed. `close` (180-192) and `remove` (195-211) are the same. Found by an audit of the shape that produced N60 and N61, and verified by reading each site.
  - **And the guard that would have stopped a second command reaches nothing.** `busy` is set and cleared in five places in that hook and **has no consumer anywhere outside it**: grepped across `src/`, the only other matches for the word are unrelated ASR and audio statuses. So nothing on screen is disabled while a project command runs, which is what makes two of them overlapping reachable in the first place, and it also means the `setBusy(false)` an older answer performs is invisible rather than harmful.
  - **What it needs, in order.** Decide whether `busy` is meant to gate the interface, because that answers whether the overlap should be prevented or merely survived. Then the counter, on `run`, `close`, `remove` and the startup restore at 230-251, guarding the failure paths as well as the success ones. The audit is in `sublore-meta/docs/transport-after-open-tasks.md`.
  - **Closed 2026-09-10, PR #194, verified on Linux.** An `asking` counter, taken before each command and read back when it answers, in `run`, `close`, `remove` and the startup restore, guarding the failure paths as well as the success ones. The restore yields to anything the user has asked for since, which it did not before. **The `busy` half is filed as N63** rather than answered here: it is a question about the greying ruling, not about correctness. Full gate clean, battery green at 75 spec files and 439 tests. As with N60 and N61, no check demonstrates the race.
- [x] **N61 Two source opens in flight land in whichever order they answer (filed 2026-09-10).** `src/hooks/useSourceFile.ts:40-64` has no generation counter at all, in `open` or in `close`, so two opens started close together apply in the order the backend answers rather than the order they were asked for, and a close answering after an open empties the column over a document that is loaded. `useVideoPlayer.ts` has exactly this discipline, an `opening.current` captured before the call and read again after it, in all six of its callbacks.
  - Found while closing N60, which was the same shape one file over. Not fixed in the same change: `useSourceFile` needs the counter introduced rather than a line added, and the behaviour when a refused open lands after a good one is a decision, not a translation of what the video hook does.
  - **What it needs.** The counter, and then the question the video hook already answers: a refused open that is no longer the current one must not put its error on screen either, which is why `useVideoPlayer` guards its `catch` as well as its success. See `sublore-meta/docs/transport-after-open-tasks.md`.
  - **Closed 2026-09-10, PR #192, verified on Linux.** `useSourceFile` takes an `asking` number before each call and reads it back when the answer arrives, in `open` and `close`, on the failure path as well as the success one. **Stated plainly: no check demonstrates it.** Two opens close enough together would need a second chooser opened and answered inside the first open's flight, which the chooser's own timing does not allow, and a demonstration resting on that would be intermittent, which is worse than none. What is corrected is the invariant `useVideoPlayer` states and keeps in all six of its calls. Full gate clean, battery green at 75 spec files and 439 tests.
- [x] **N60 The transport offers Play while the clock is moving (filed 2026-09-09).** Measured on Linux in the environment `silent-machine.spec.js` runs in, where every audio backend goes nowhere: the position passes three seconds and keeps climbing, and for fifteen seconds `.controls__button` never stops saying "Play". So the state the button reads and the position the clock reads disagree. In one run out of six, with a probe that added four seconds of readings before the assertion, the label was right immediately, so it is not a fixed lag.
  - **Why it matters on its own.** Someone looking at that button believes the video is stopped while it is playing, and the one control they would reach for does the opposite of what they expect. It is the same class as N28, where the playhead and the position disagreed.
  - **What it needs.** Where `paused` comes from, traced against where the position comes from: `useVideoPlayer.ts` holds both, and whether mpv's `pause` property and its `time-pos` reach the page through the same path is the question. Whether it happens with a working audio device is not yet known, and that is the first measurement: every existing spec asserts the label right after a click and passes, so if it is confined to the silent machine that narrows it a long way. Found while landing N13's fix (PR #189).
  - **First two measurements, 2026-09-09, verified on Linux.** **With a working audio device the label agrees after 44 milliseconds**, measured by running the same spec with the silent environment switched off. Without one it never agrees within fifteen seconds. So this is confined to a machine with no audio output, which narrows it to the same family as N13 and means no existing spec could have caught it.
  - **Where to look, from reading the code.** `setPaused` in `src/hooks/useVideoPlayer.ts:159` writes the belief the button draws **only** when `invoke("video_play")` resolves *and* `opening.current` has not moved since the call. So the label is stuck for one of two reasons: the command rejected, or the open generation changed under it. The app's own log for those runs carries no refusal and no warning, and `AoInitFailed` never arrived either, which is the fallback working. **The next probe is to put the error code on screen in that state**, or to report the generation, because those two are what tell the two branches apart.
  - **Narrowed to the frontend 2026-09-10, verified on Linux, six runs out of six.** It is not intermittent on that machine: the label never agrees within ten seconds, and with a working device it agrees in 44 milliseconds. **The backend is not at fault**, measured with probes inside `player.rs`: `set_pause(false)` returns ok in 64 ms, the `pause` property change to false arrives, the state lock is not poisoned, and `state now says paused=false`. **And the page receives it**: a probe recording every `video://state` event on `window` caught `state event paused=false status=ready`, while an attribute carrying what the button actually drew still read `paused=true`. So the defect is between `setState(event.payload)` in `useVideoPlayer.ts` and the render of `VideoControls`, and nowhere else.
  - Ruled out by reading: `useVideoPlayer` is instantiated once (`src/App.tsx:463`), `paused` reaches the component as `state.paused` with nothing in between, and `opening.current` moves only in `open` and `close`, neither of which runs during that click. **Next probe:** record every `setState` call with its value and the value at every render, which tells a render that never happened from one that happened and was put back. Every probe used here was removed and the battery is green at 74 spec files.
  - **Correction, 2026-09-10: it is not confined to the machine with no audio device, and the entry above said it was.** The control was run again on the current build with the silent environment switched off: **the label never agrees there either**, two runs out of two, the same as two out of two with it on. The earlier 44 millisecond reading was taken during the same session against a tree that was being changed between runs and is not reproducible; it is struck rather than trusted.
  - **Closed 2026-09-10, verified on Linux, PR #190.** The cause, taken from the app's own record: `useVideoPlayer`'s `open` wrote `paused: true` when `video_open` resolved, and the backend emits the ready state **before** that call returns, which is the event that puts the transport on screen. So there is a window where the button is drawn and clickable and the open's promise is still in flight, and a Play pressed inside it was overwritten when the promise landed. The probe caught the order exactly: `event paused=false`, `command resolved paused=false`, then `open resolved, writing paused=true` last, with every render after it drawing Play while the clock ran.
  - The fix is that `open` keeps the `paused` the state already has: the field belongs to the backend, which emits it at the open and on every change. **Proved by mutation:** putting `paused: true` back reddens the new check in three runs out of three. `transport-after-open.spec.js` presses Play at the earliest moment the transport exists, and asserts the button and the clock together. Battery green twice at 75 spec files, 439 tests. Criteria in `sublore-meta/docs/transport-after-open-tasks.md`.
  - **What the shape now looks like: it is directional.** `video-surface.spec.js:261` clicks the transport to **pause** and waits for the button to read "Play", and that passes in every run. This entry's own flow clicks to **play** and the button never reads "Pause". So a pause reaches the button and a play does not, in this flow, which is a much narrower thing to look for than a label that lags.

Yet in CI the pause test sometimes finds the transport reading "Play", meaning stopped, and its click then started playback instead of stopping it. Still open on this entry's other criterion.** mpv's `--audio-fallback-to-null` defaults to no: when no audio device can be opened, mpv **ends the file** rather than playing it silently, and the device is opened once playback starts producing audio, about a second in on a loaded machine. That is the four recorded positions, 1.03, 1.07, 1.13 and 0.7 of sixty. Reproduced twice against mpv v0.41.0 directly: without the option, "finished playback, audio output initialization failed (reason 4)" and exit 2; with it, the null output opens and the file plays to the end. `SAFE_OPTIONS` now sets it. Numbers in `sublore-meta/docs/silent-machine-tasks.md`.

So CI is the instrument: this closes when the line stops appearing there.

  - **What the line has reported, 2026-09-03.** Four occurrences across eight green CI runs, and the positions are all the same: 1.03, 1.07, 1.13 and 0.7 seconds of 60.023. So playback does not fade out somewhere along the file — it starts and dies about a second in. The fixture is `sample.mkv`, which is 60.023 s long, so this is nowhere near its end.
  - Refuted by reading the code: nothing in the app pauses on the way through. The test before this one never pauses — it clicks Play, waits for the clock, hides and shows the surface, and waits for the clock again — and `set_property("pause", true)` appears in exactly two places, opening a file and the pause command itself.
  - Does not reproduce here: six runs of the spec on this machine, no occurrence. Same shape as N11 and N14, both of which needed contention before they would show, and both of which were then named in one run of it. That reproduction is the next step and it belongs to a night.
  - Instrumented meanwhile: mpv pausing itself now says so, with the position, in the app's own log — which CI has collected since #64. Until this, an unasked-for pause left no trace anywhere, which is why four CI runs could see it and say nothing about it.
  - AC unchanged: the cause is named before the reporting is removed. Deleting the line because the suite is green would be closing the entry by looking away.
  - **Recurred here on 2026-09-11, and for the first time the log answers the question this entry was opened for.** One battery of N148's five came back red on `transport-after-open.spec.js` with this entry's own signature, `timed out waiting for the transport button to read "Pause"`, and N96's work made the reading possible: the failure now names what it saw, `it reads "Play" and the clock is at 0`. The kept log is four lines of open and one line more:
    - `12:13:39 video: the picture is drawn 640 by 360`
    - `12:13:39 waveform: job 2 finished in 262 ms`
    - `12:13:44 playback: asked mpv to play, and it took it`
  - **The three candidates are all ruled out.** There is exactly one `playback:` line, so one click produced one command; it says mpv took it, so mpv neither lost it nor refused it; and the spec clicks once and waits five seconds, which is where the line landed. The command was not lost, not refused, and the button had nothing to redraw yet.

The five seconds are therefore **before** the IPC boundary: the click, the handler, `runCommand`, `togglePlayback`, `invoke`. That is a narrowing this entry has never had.

The spec is green three out of three alone on the same build and the three batteries after it are green, so it is not N148's change.
- [x] **N11 One SIGSEGV on exit in `mpv-context`, on a branch that touches no teardown code (filed 2026-09-02).** Running the raw check battery for T5 on a loaded machine, `pnpm e2e:mpv-context` failed with `exit {"code":null,"signal":"SIGSEGV"}` after its first three assertions passed, so the crash is on the way out rather than a behavioural failure. Six sequential reruns immediately afterwards were 6 green, 0 red, and the branch it was seen on changes only `src/` and `e2e/`, no Rust and nothing in the teardown path, so it is not that branch's defect.
  - It is filed rather than attached to N1b, which is closed. N1b was the close gate's **save** branch, measured at 2 in 30 under six concurrent streams and explicitly not reproducible sequentially; this was a different check, on the plain exit path, and one occurrence is not a rate. What the two share is the shape: a crash after the work is done, on the way out, with load the visible difference. N1b's own status line already carries a correction saying the delivered binary was run three times sequentially rather than the thirty its criterion asked for, so the evidence that closed it was thinner than the checkbox suggests.
  - The load at the time was real and is worth naming: the machine's one-minute average was above 20 while the battery ran, on 16 cores.
  - **Second sighting, 2026-09-03, in CI.** `pnpm e2e:scale` on the run that removed the D-Bus stall: all four of its assertions passed and the process then died on the way out, `exits [{"code":null,"signal":"SIGSEGV"},{"code":0,"signal":null}]`. Two occurrences is still not a rate, but it is no longer one, and it happened on the first run where startup was 29 seconds shorter, so teardown now follows startup much sooner. That is a variable worth naming when this is reproduced.
  - **Third sighting 2026-09-03 on #64, both launches at once**: `exits [{"code":null,"signal":"SIGSEGV"},{"code":null,"signal":"SIGSEGV"}]`.
  - **Reproduced, named and closed, 2026-09-03.** It does not reproduce on an idle machine; under concurrent load it does, and `pnpm e2e:n11-battery` is that load in the repository rather than a shell loop nobody else can run. Fifty runs in five streams: **6 closed on a signal before the change, 0 after**, and the six runs that had to be killed at teardown went to zero with them.
  - The frame is `_gdk_x11_display_queue_events`, identical in four core dumps at `libgdk-3.so.0.2420.32 +0x7271a`. The faulting instruction is `cmp (%rax),%rsi` right after `mov (%rcx),%rax` and a null check, which is `G_TYPE_CHECK_INSTANCE_TYPE` inlined: the object's class pointer is not null and is not mapped either. A GObject freed under GDK while it is still walking its window list.
  - Which object was found by taking it away. `Surface::destroy` called `gdk_window_destroy` and then dropped the last reference to the wrapper. Not destroying the window at all: 0 core dumps in 50 launches, but 3 runs left a process behind, so that trade is a crash for an orphan. Destroying the window and keeping the wrapper alive: 0 core dumps and no orphans, which is the change made.
  - Ruled out with a measurement each: the waveform fallback landed in #64 (17 dumps in 50 launches before it, 21 after, 0.8 standard errors apart), and the teardown order, which the code already gets right — mpv is shut down first and the surface only goes if it did.
  - Left as an observation rather than a claim: under a much heavier load than the battery's — the check pinned to two cores with a busy loop contending them — 2 or 3 runs in 25 leave a process behind, and they do so before the change and after it (0, 2, 3 and 2 across four arms of 25). The battery's own six hangs go to zero with the crashes, so the two instruments do not agree, and starving the app to a third of two cores is not a condition CI is in. Whether that is one fault or two needs its own measurement before it gets its own entry. If it cannot be reproduced in a battery sized to catch a defect at the rate N1b was measured at, that number is reported and this is closed as unreproduced rather than as fixed.
  - **Ticked 2026-09-09, verified on Linux by reading the tree.** The entry has said "reproduced, named and closed" since 2026-09-03 and the box was never ticked. The change it describes is in `src-tauri/src/video/surface/linux.rs:82-89`: `destroy` destroys the X window and `std::mem::forget`s the wrapper, with the reason in the comment beside it. **The orphan observation in the row above moves to N16**, which is the entry that owns a process left behind; it is not a second half of this one.

- [x] **N10 Thirty seconds of D-Bus before every CI launch, and none on the owner's machine (filed 2026-09-02, measured 2026-09-03).** Between GTK initialising and `Sublore 0.1.0 starting on linux` reaching the log, the eleven pid-matched launches of CI run 33639531468 spent 13.08, 27.06, 28.67, 29.34, 29.45, 29.49, 29.57, 29.81, 29.86, 29.96 and 29.97 seconds, so this entry's original "between 28.7 and 30.0 across eleven launches" was wrong about its own run and is corrected here rather than quietly dropped. Across 676 launches in 31 jobs between 2026-08-30 and 2026-09-02 the same interval never exceeds 30.17 s, with a median of 30.06 and a mode of 30.06 plus or minus 0.01, and two launches seconds apart in one step on one runner measure 6.33 s and 30.06 s. That is a fixed timeout, not a loaded machine. It costs roughly 630 of that job's 1030 seconds and 73 per cent of the 1226 s job on run 33690642329, and it scales one for one with a check list that has nearly tripled since the first CI run.
  - Measured on the owner's machine 2026-09-03, verified on Linux, with the existing debug build: exec to the first log line is 0.238 to 0.243 s, and the CI-shaped interval from GTK's dbind warning to that line is 0.068 s under CI's own `xvfb-run -a -s "-screen 0 1920x1080x24"` and 0.243 s on the real display, 0.233 s with the NVIDIA workarounds armed. The release binary puts its window on screen at 0.205 s. The first AC is met: the pre-log phase is two orders of magnitude inside CONTRIBUTING.md §7's 2 s, this is a CI cost and not a product defect, and the entry's stated cause, Xvfb plus software rendering, is struck because the local Xvfb run reproduces the identical DRI3 software fallback and costs 68 ms.
  - What waits, from a dbus-monitor capture of the pre-log phase on this machine: `GetNameOwner org.gnome.SessionManager`, `GetNameOwner org.xfce.SessionManager`, then `AddMatch` and `StartServiceByName` for `org.freedesktop.portal.Desktop` on the `org.freedesktop.portal.Inhibit` interface. That is GtkApplication's session tracking; all three names are in libgtk-3 and none is in libwebkit2gtk-4.1, so the waiter is `app.register(cancellable)` at tao-0.35.3 `event_loop.rs:229`, reached from tauri `app.rs:2338-2342` inside `build()`, before the window at `app.rs:2523` and before the log line at `src-tauri/src/lib.rs:167`. The logs agree: in CI and in the local reproduction the libEGL lines land after the wait, so the stall is in front of EGL and the webview, not inside them.
  - The mechanism is proved on this machine, both directions. With a session bus where `org.freedesktop.portal.Desktop` is declared activatable by a `.service` file whose Exec never claims the name, the same binary takes 30.239 s to its first log line, 30.073 s of it between the dbind warning and libEGL, which is CI's mode to within 13 ms, printing CI's lines in CI's order including the `org.a11y.Bus` ServiceUnknown warning at 0.135 s. Delete that one file on the same bus with the same config: 0.223 s. Against a bus with no service directory at all: 0.229 s.
  - It is not proved to be CI's instance and is written down as a lead. `xdg-desktop-portal` 1.18.4 and `xdg-desktop-portal-gtk` 1.15.1 arrive on the runner as dependencies of `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev`, visible in the e2e job's own apt output, so the name is activatable there while nothing can claim it in a headless job with no session, and every stalled CI launch gets an error reply from a live bus at the moment its stall begins. Nobody has moved that file aside in a CI run, which is the only thing that would settle it.
  - Killed with a measurement each: the NVIDIA hatch (0.233 s armed against 0.243 s disarmed, and `e2e/lib/env.js:31` pins `SUBLORE_WEBKIT_WORKAROUNDS=0` on both machines, so CI and the harness always take the same branch and only the printed word differs); the accessibility bus in its ServiceUnknown form (CI's line verbatim against an answering bus with no service files, 0.229 s); libEGL DRI3 (printed in every fast run too); process start and dynamic linking (the no-display refusal at `src-tauri/src/main.rs:90` is a 0.59 s step). Also killed as CI's cause: `dbus-launch`'s autolaunch retry loop, six forks each ending in a 3.000 s `clock_nanosleep`, which is real here (18.292 to 18.315 s) and was removed and restored on one permission bit, but which only runs when no bus answers, and CI's bus answers.
  - Not explained by anything above: the 34 of 151 launches that escape at 5.3 to 28.2 s and recur at the same positions in the job, the 13.08 s launch included.
  - Machine note, not the app's defect and left alone: `/home/alcahest/.dbus` is root owned and mode 700 on the owner's machine, so any application that has to autolaunch its own session bus there pays 18.3 s at startup.
  - **Proved in CI, 2026-09-03, and closed.** The mechanism was reproduced on the runner itself and not only here: one job ran the shutdown check twice, once with `/usr/share/dbus-1/services/org.freedesktop.portal.Desktop.service` moved aside and once with it in place, everything else identical. `shutdown-no-portal: exit 0 after 0m02s` against `shutdown: exit 0 after 0m31s`. That is the 29 seconds, on CI's own hardware, so the attribution that this entry called a lead is now a measurement. The file is moved aside for the whole job, before the fixtures, and the app is unchanged: it reaches its first log line in 0.24 s on real hardware and dodging a runner's condition from inside `src-tauri` would make the harness drive a configuration no user has.
  - AC (met 2026-09-03): the interval is measured on the owner's machine and reported with its number. It is under 2 s, so by this entry's own terms the budget question closes and what is left is CI cost.
  - AC: the cause in CI is named by a CI run that removes it and a CI run that has it, not by inference from a local reproduction. The cheapest form is one step that prints `ls /usr/share/dbus-1/services` and one check run with `/usr/share/dbus-1/services/org.freedesktop.portal.Desktop.service` moved aside, compared against the same interval in the other steps of the same job.
  - AC: nothing is added to `src-tauri` or to `e2e/lib/env.js` to work around a runner condition. The app is 0.24 s for a user, and a harness that arms a hatch to make CI faster would be testing a configuration no user has.
  - AC: whatever the answer, the check that proves it is a measurement with its own number in the log, not a pass or fail against a figure somebody chose.

- [x] **N9 Assertions anchored to something the environment decides (filed 2026-09-02, closed 2026-09-09).** M3.7 is one member of a class, and the class was read out in full before it produced any more CI runs. The five failures of 2026-09-01 and 2026-09-02 — the picker's pixel coordinate, the 600 ms stall inherited by fourteen ffmpeg tests, a directory named with a newline and a quote, the `asr.spec.js` chain, and a `fixtures/video` listing pinned to a literal array — are one defect: an assertion whose truth depends on a quantity the machine chooses rather than on the behaviour under test. The audit is in the planning archive as `docs/environment-anchored-assertions.md`, with every claim carrying a path and a line, and it is ranked by cost: a test that goes red on a slow runner costs an hour, and a test that goes green while the code is broken costs a release. Seventeen of the first kind and eleven of the second, plus what to leave alone and why, because several of these can only be checked by driving a real display and saying so is part of the answer.
  - The five that matter most, all of them silent. `crates/sublore-asr/tests/no_network.rs:85-98` constructs a `NeverFetcher` and never passes it to `ordinary_workflow`, so the assertion that nothing reached the transport cannot fail under any change to the product; the criterion is genuinely covered by the socket-counting test beneath it, so what is lost is the second proof the header promises. `crates/sublore-io/tests/atomic_save.rs:549-557` and `:608-616` return green with zero assertions whenever the process can write into a `0o500` directory, which is what root gets, and the `eprintln!` that says so is swallowed by libtest — two of CONTRIBUTING.md section 3's own criteria, silently unverified, and a skip section 5.4 forbids. `crates/sublore-asr/src/sidecar.rs:759-763` asserts `(1..=8)` around a function that clamps to `(1, MAX_THREADS)` with `MAX_THREADS = 8`. `e2e/scripts/n1b-load-battery.js:77-87` answers "no core dump" when `coredumpctl` is not installed, which is half of N1b's closing criterion satisfied by a missing package. `e2e/scripts/n1b-load-probe.js:37` still clicks `{750, 540}` where `e2e/lib/paths.js:37` records `{840, 523}` for the same window after T1 moved it, and `:80` still waits the 3500 ms that `close-gate-check.js:162` records as having failed the first real CI run.
  - Two findings change what is at risk rather than what is broken. `.github/workflows/ci.yml:155-156` runs `cargo test --workspace` on `windows-latest`, so the Rust suite runs there and every Windows filesystem rule in it is live today, not MW.1b's problem — which is what the newline-and-quote failure was. And there is no frontend test runner at all: no vitest, no jest, no `*.test.*` under `src/`, so every line of TypeScript is covered by the E2E suite or by nothing, which is why the E2E suite's silent failures cost more here than they would elsewhere.
  - The undiscovered sibling of the fifth CI failure is `src-tauri/tests/subtitle_commands.rs:270-282`, which lists `fixtures/subtitles/srt/clean` and fails on any entry that is not a `.srt`. Adding a README there turns it red for a reason unrelated to a save writing beside a source. The repository already holds the right shape in four places — a floor plus a relationship, never an inventory (`subtitle_commands.rs:85-89`, `formats/tests/common/mod.rs:56-61`, `edit/tests/mutation.rs:78-80`, `formats/tests/ass_roundtrip.rs:114-132`).
  - AC: the seven changes the audit's own closing section names are made, each as itself, and each is shown to fail before it is shown to pass. A test that could not fail before the change must be made to fail by breaking what it claims to cover, and the run that proves it is recorded.
  - AC: no assertion is weakened and no test is skipped to close this. Where an environment genuinely cannot express a case, the run refuses by name with an actionable sentence, the way `e2e/lib/paths.js:44-49` and `wayland-attach-check.js:53-71` already do, rather than returning green.
  - AC: the two remaining hardcoded pixel constants (`e2e/lib/paths.js:37`, `e2e/scripts/picker-thread-check.js:70-72`) each carry a comment saying how the number was measured and against which window state, not only that it will need re-measuring. `paths.js:37` is already the model; `picker-thread-check.js` is not.

- [x] **N59 A fractional device ratio is reachable under Xvfb after all (filed 2026-09-09).** `e2e/scripts/scaled-surface-check.js`'s header says a fractional ratio cannot be produced in the harness: "`Xft.dpi` through `xrdb` does not reach WebKitGTK without an XSETTINGS manager", and concludes that N2c's criterion is met on the owner's 1.5 display "and nowhere else". With an XSETTINGS manager it is reached. Measured on Linux 2026-09-09 with `xsettingsd` publishing `Gdk/WindowScalingFactor 1` and `Xft/DPI`: at 96 dpi the window is 1024x700 and the surface 346x286+114+69; at 144 dpi the window is **still** 1024x700 and the surface is 445x429+172+102. The window does not move and the surface does, which is exactly N2c's shape.
  - **What this is worth.** N2c is the defect where the page's rectangle has to be resolved to native pixels before it crosses the IPC boundary, and the regression it nearly shipped put the surface at the ratio squared. Today nothing on Linux can catch that coming back. This can.
  - **What it needs.** The strong assertion is the page's own rectangle in CSS pixels times `devicePixelRatio` against the X geometry, which needs a webdriver rather than a script: a relative comparison between two ratios is muddied by the layout reflowing at the new ratio. So the shape is a spec with its own display and its own settings manager, not another entry in `package.json`. It also adds `xsettingsd` as a harness dependency, MIT and packaged everywhere, which the E2E CI job would have to install.
  - Found while attempting N58. The header sentence it contradicts should be corrected in the same change that lands the spec, not before: it is true of `xrdb`, which is what it names.
  - **Closed 2026-09-09, verified on Linux, PR #186.** `e2e/specs/fractional-ratio.spec.js` starts a settings manager holding the ratio the display already has, asserts the invariant at 1, publishes 144 dpi, and asserts it again at 1.5. The assertion is layout independent: the surface's geometry in X is the stage's own rectangle in CSS pixels times `devicePixelRatio`. **Proved by mutation with N2c itself:** with the ratio forced to 1 in `VideoStage.tsx` the fractional check goes red and the ratio-1 check stays green, which is the right pair, because the defect is invisible at a ratio of 1. The battery is green three runs in a row at 73 spec files and 435 tests, and the spec restores the display in an `after` hook that fails loudly rather than leaving the worker's later specs drawing at the wrong size. `xsettingsd` is now a harness dependency and the E2E CI job installs it.
- [x] **N58 Nothing exercises the video stage's device-ratio listeners (filed 2026-09-09).** `src/components/VideoStage.tsx:56-66` listens on three `min-resolution` media queries so the native surface's region is measured again when the display's scale factor changes under a running app. Neither the `ResizeObserver` nor the window `resize` fires in that case, which is why the listeners exist, and no check covers them: `pnpm e2e:scale` sets `GDK_SCALE` before the app starts, so the ratio never moves while it is running. Without them a surface would keep a region measured at the old ratio, which is a picture in the wrong place and the wrong size.
  - **Attempted 2026-09-09 and the instrument does not fit, verified on Linux.** A rig was built and run: `xsettingsd` publishing `Gdk/WindowScalingFactor` under Xvfb, the app started at factor 1, the factor moved to 2 with a SIGHUP. It passed 5 of 5 on a sane build, then **stayed green with the listeners removed**, mutation in the build. The numbers say why: without the listeners the surface is *exactly* double (346x286+114+69 becomes 692x572+228+138), because GDK rescales a logical geometry on its own; with them the page measures again and lands on 690x574+230+138. Two pixels apart, inside any sensible tolerance. **Under an integer factor the listeners are not what makes this work**, so the rig cannot prove them and the draft was not committed. They matter for a fractional ratio, where GTK's factor stays 1 and the ratio arrives as page zoom. See `sublore-meta/docs/ratio-change-tasks.md`.
  - **Closed 2026-09-09 with an answer rather than a check, verified on Linux: no rig that exists can exercise them, and the measurement says why.** Two paths were built and both were run with the listeners removed and the mutation confirmed in the build. **The integer path** (`xsettingsd` moving `Gdk/WindowScalingFactor` from 1 to 2): the surface still lands correctly, because GDK rescales a logical geometry on its own. **The fractional path** (`fractional-ratio.spec.js` at 144 dpi, which is now a real check, N59): the surface still lands correctly, because a higher ratio means fewer CSS pixels in the same window, the viewport changes size in CSS terms, and the `ResizeObserver` fires. So the comment beside the listeners is wrong for this path: the CSS box does not stay where it was.
  - **What that does and does not say.** In both reachable cases another listener gets there first, so removing these three changes nothing observable. That is not proof they are dead: a ratio change with no CSS size change at all, a window moved between two displays of the same logical size and different scale, is a case Xvfb cannot produce and neither can `xsettingsd`. **They are not deleted on this evidence**, and nothing claims they are covered. If a rig for that case ever exists, this is what it would be for.
  - What it needs is a display whose scale factor changes under a running app: an XSETTINGS rig that flips `Gdk/WindowScalingFactor` on the running server, asserting the surface's geometry before and after against the same CSS box. Xvfb will take an XSETTINGS manager, so this does not need the owner's own session, but it does need one written.

Types and constants only, and nothing in the workspace depends on it yet — the fixture module is N8b and the loader is N8c.
  - AC: adding, reordering or widening a field in any `#[repr(C)]` type fails `cargo test -p sublore-module-api` and names the type, and so does a table whose declared size stops matching its own `size_of`.
  - AC: a string with a NUL in its middle crosses the boundary whole, because a cue can hold one: `SourceText::from_bytes` scans only the first 1024 bytes of a file for NULs, so a C string on the boundary would truncate that cue.
  - Status 2026-09-02, verified-by-tests **on Linux**, compiled for `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`: ten unit tests, and both pins were shown to fail by widening two structs before being restored. This crate has no behaviour in the app to verify and no in-app criteria of its own; the boundary's behavioural criteria are the loader's and land with N8c.

The three pinned sizes moved to 64, 40 and 128, which are the numbers the document had computed, so the design and the compiler agree.
  - **The layout pin did not hold what N8a said it held, and a mutation is what said so.** Its criterion reads "adding, reordering or widening a field in any `#[repr(C)]` type fails `cargo test` and names the type". Widening `SubloreLine::flags` from `u32` to `u64` failed nothing: the four extra bytes land in padding that was already there, so the size stays 64 and the alignment stays 8. An offset pin was added and **still let it through**, because every one of the six offsets is also unchanged. Two fields of the same width swapped is a second case the size pin cannot see and the offset pin does catch.
  - **What holds it now:** a never-called function that destructures every table and binds each field to its declared type. A field added, removed or renamed stops the destructuring compiling; one whose type changes stops the binding compiling. That is the only one of the three that catches a field widened into its own padding, which is a real ABI difference a closed module would be miscompiled against in silence.

All four were corrected in the planning archive on 2026-09-01, against the code as it stands: `d224f3c` derives the surface's visibility, so the two calls those addresses named have one caller each now. T1 has landed.


Goal: the free product's core: cue list, text editing, timing adjust against waveform, side-by-side source/target view.

Rebuild the shell around a panel layout with the project rail on the left, the video beside it taking the full height of the top block, waveform and current-line band stacked to its right, and the cue grid across the bottom, with a modern finish, per `shell-layout.md` and `shell-mockup.html`. The engine is not touched: this is the frontend shell plus the file-dialog commands.

T1 landed on 2026-09-01. The design it implements is `shell-layout.md` and `shell-mockup.html`; the criteria below are the milestone's, the breakdown's are each task's.
  - **T2 landed 2026-09-01, verified on Linux.** The one-column stack is the five regions the layout names — chrome, rail, video, tools column, grid — with the status bar of decision 24 A1 across the bottom outside them, carrying the document line and the unsaved marker on the left and the transient messages and errors on the right. `App.css` is split per region under `src/styles/`, over a dark-only colour token layer (decision 22). The tools column is empty until the waveform (M2.4) and the current line (T5) arrive in it; the chrome region holds the old command bars until T3 and T4 replace them.

It is not a layer, deliberately: an HTML layer over the video is neither visible nor clickable until decision 1's occlusion lands (T8), measured here — a box on the stage rectangle took no click at all, while the same box over the grid took every one. The menu item is in Edit because File has no room for it: `chrome.spec.js` walks Open video down to Quit over the disabled pair, and `quit-gate-check.js` reaches Quit as the last enabled item in File, so an item anywhere in File breaks one of the two. It waits there for the Audio title (decision 24 A2).
